A PRIEST'S VOTE.

The Rev Michael Dowley, assistant priest at the Church of the Sacred Hrart, claimed a vote as joint occupier of the presbytery in Liverpool• Road The Barrister : On what conditions do you reside in the house? Are your board and residence part of your stipend? Claimant : My position is this. I am sent by the bishop of the diocese to the presbytery and it is on his authority that I have residence there. The Barrister : You have that authority without the consent of the Rector ?—Yes. He also resides there ?—Yes. _ _ . . . .

That privilege, of course, is in addition to any stipend you get ?—Yes. Is the Bishop the only person who has authority with regard to that Rectory house ?—Yes. Is it in the hands of trustees, s I whom he is one ?—I think that is so. It is freehold and there is co rent payable on cur part. It bag been bought out. The Rector pays the rats, and taxes in his name. lam sup- posed to contribute to them and indirectly I have to find ratea and taxes jointly with him. I have a salary from the Workhouse Hospital as chaplain, and of course it goes to him and he pays rates and taxes through these monies. .

Mr. Hayes pointed out that Father Rockilif was already on the list in respect of the house and there was no mention of a joint tenancy. Mr. Woodcock (assistant overseer) said the value of the house was sufficient for two vote.. . .

In reply to Mr. Hayes, the claimant said the Bishop bad power to send him elsewhere at any time, but he was generally mil) transferred once in live or six years.' The Bishop had equal power over the Rector. Father Rockliff and he (claimant) were the only secular priests in Warrington, and he supposed that was the reason why the other priests had not claimed votes—they were members of religions orders. The Banister : Has the Rector power to move you ? Claimant : He has not. . .

T.13-eitt7l;iate;-:- The only question is whether this is not, after all, a service franchise, because the person who l,us authority, under whom you serve, is the Bishop. It seems to me it is a service franchise in both cases. Mr. New : Would not that apply in the case of the Rector who is already on division one The Barrister t He may claim for other reasons. He is in possession of a livi g of which he cannot be deprived, except under special circumstancrs. Mr. New : My contention is that this gentleman Ims the same status ss the Rector. He is independent of the Rector and no-one but the Bishop has power to remove him.

The Barrister : The moment you make him indepen- dent of the Rector, don't you make him occupy the premises by virtue of service, the person under shorn he serves beiog the Bishop nho dots not reside on the premises? Mr. New said he thought that also applied to the Ecclesiastical Commitsioners and to mauy Church of England benefices._ . .

the Barrister : The Rector's occupation may be, in a sense, independent possession. 'Bre question of rates crops up in this case. I don't want to rake the point but there it is. _

Claimant : May I se) that all persons of my status in the Liverpool district have votes and I was on the register at ‘Vidnes in respect of a similiar claim to this. The Barrister : I see cases in the books where apparently it has been allowed but, for the life of me, cannot undeistand on what basis. However, I think I shall allow this claim. lithe matter crops up again, I shall certainly go more carefully into it. I have some little doubt, but twill allow it. (To Mr. Hayes) : You don't press the question of rates? Mr. Hales: 140 sir. .. .

The Warrington Compel y of tLe West Lancashire Transport and Supply Column left their headquarters on Saturday for a week-end camp at Knutsford. Tents were pitched in the Knutsford Show field on Saturday evening and soon the cooks were busy making puddings by moonlight ready for Sunday's dinner. All food and other necessary camp equipage was carried on the transport waggons. Two sosp'cious looking beer barrels were conspicuous but they only cont iined 36 gallons of drinking water. OPEN-AIR POLITICS.

THE LIBERAL GOVERNMENT'S WORK FOR THE PEOPLE.

Mr. New and His Critics.

The successful open-air campaign whioh the Warrington Progressive Labour League has conducted during the summer months is GOV/ drawing to a close, but it is intended to hold indoor meetings In the winter. Public interest in the League's propaganda has been splendidly maintained and each Monday has found a large and enthusiastic crowd gathered round the plat- form at Bridge Foot. There wee another interesting end highly encouraging meeting on Monday evening when Councillor Middleton, of Oldham, was the chief ape her. Mr. .1. MORTON, who presided, said they were there to propagate Liberaliam, which they telieved to be the "jam " for the working-classes. The Liber.l Govern- ment was working pre-eminently in the interests of the labouring classes and it seemed to him extremely sad that at the present juncture, when there were so many upheavals sod

ATTEmi.rs ON THE ['ART OF THR WORKING ( LASSES TO GAIN BETTER CONDITION', that they in Warrington were tied down very largely In the discussion of the fiscal question. The Borough Member had initiated a sort of campaign which seemed to him (the speaker) to be absolutely absurd. They all know how businesa w in conductad and that any business man, whether Liberal, Conservative, or Labour, had to deal with men on business lines, and to buy in the cheapest market. They were, however, being told by some of their friends that they should etiy from their own people in Warrington simply because they were giving labour apparently to Warrington people. They could not conduct business on philanthropic lines or they would soon come to grief, and he did not think the workingdosses as a whole would swallow what the tariff " reformers" told them on that subject. (Applause). Councillor MIDDLETON began his speech by con- gratulating the %Warrington Liberal Association upon the excellent provision it had made for open-air propaganda. .0 purchasing that admirable van they had saown an igaterprise which other Liberal asiocia- tions would do well to copy. ([tear hear). Ten years ago politics were talked about for a few mouths in the winter tirue. Now they lasted all the year round, and the curious thing was that people took an interest in them all the time. The reason was that the politics of to-day had come right down isto the homes and doily lives of the common people. John Bright declared that in every country they found the real life of the nation in the cottage and if the light of their legislation did not shine in there, it was a failure and a mistake. That expressed the reason for the extra- ordinary interest that was at present being taken in politics. Ike

was shining into the homes of the people and would continui to do so. (Applause.) We bad had proof lately of the existence of the " common " man—the man who got his living by the labour of his hands and the sweat of his brow. We were too often in the habit of looking upon the so.called unskilled workman as somebody of no account. Anybody, it was said, could do that class of work. That, however, was a totally erroneous idea. The " common " man had shown his importance—and he (the speaker) rejoiced at it—by giving up work fora few &vs. Ile had proved that he was capable, by merely sitting still, of upsetting all our social arrangements and of interfering with our food supply. ALDERMAN JOHN EVANS

An Old Athlete and a Municipal

Worker.

The current issue of " The Licensing World and Licensed Trade Ite•iew," contains a photogregh of Alderman John Evans, a member of the general council of the Licensed Victuallers* Defence League, and also la view of his establishment, the Ball's Head, Church- street, Warrington. Alderman Evans (states an accompanying biographical sketch) was born on November 12th, 1511, at the Ballu Head Hotel, and is therefore in his sisty-seventh year. Ihe family have occupied the same premises for four generations, and the aldermen entered into possession in February, 18i. Ile was educated at Spring House Academy, and served his apprenticeship as an engineer with Mr. K. Kitchen. Scotland Bank Ironworks, Scotland-road, and was after- wards employed es shop foreman for tla Messrs. Pearson and Knowles Coal and Iron Company. Limited. From an early age he took considerable interest in athletics, and at the Warrington Sports held In 1848 won not only the 100 yards race but the 120 yards hurdle race and the running high jump, tie running long jump, and the sparring and gymnastic competitions ; and the alderman can yet hold his own in the matter of self-defence. In the second standing jump competition he was besten by Alderman William Pierpoiut, but he obtainei the medal for being the best athlete in the club. He has worked hard to pro- mote the success of the Warrington Football Club, and be was a member of the council for several years and president for one or two years. Ile was appointed the first president of the Warrington and District Bowling Association, and in this c inneetion it may be interest- ing to note that the Bull's Head club now holds the championship of the Larceshire end Cheshire Bowling Association, as well as the championship of the 'War- rington and District Bowling Association. Ile bas been President of the Warrington Licensed Victuallers' Association for the last nineteen years, and Is also vice-president of the No. 3 District of the Licensed Victuallers Defence League, and a member of the general council. Ile is one of theoriginal members of the Gymnsaium Committee, and has been in office since the erection of the building, so that it will be seen he is a great believer in the old Latin motto, " Mens sans in corpora moo." His connection wish the Council had been a useful one. Elected a member in August, Is9l, for the Howley Ward (beating Mr. James Bat tersby by one of the largest majorities ever recorded , he entered upon his municipal duties with considerable seat, and on July sth. 1904, succeeded the late Mr. Robert Heaton as an alderman of the borough. lie wns Deputy-Chairman of the Cemetery and Sanitary Works Committees in November, 1895, being subsequently appointed chsirmin of inch. He is now Chairman ct the Water Committee and Deputy-Chairman of the Caw Committee, having resigned the position of clisirman of the Sanitary Works Committee after seven years' service, owiag to the new Standing Order a bich was passed on November 9th last. On his resignation a special vote of thanks was pessed by the committee recorded on the minutes, and no illuminated copy pre- sented to the alderman. Ile is a member of several other committees, and his technical knowledge v.ith respect to engineering has been of extreme value to the Corporation. Mr. Evans is not actively associated with any political party and poses as an Indevendent. He is much respected for his straightforward, msnly, end independent attitude, and has served his native town faithfully and well.

Notes by " Ovalist. '

With the arrival of the Kangaroos this week, the question of Frawley is coming to the front. Quite possibly it may create something of a sensation in N.C. circles before it is settled. Already there have been s'atements published in Manchester newspapers which not only convey a d','orted view of the facts, but indicate that our Colonial visitors will not relinquish the player without some trouble.

There is no question now ns to his being with the 'touring party. As a matter of fact he has been provisionally selected to play in the oneniog match at •Coveotry neat Saturday. A quotation from the Australian " Sun" in another column gives details of Frawley's rend-off and a letter in the " Sporting Chronicle " of Thursday from one of the tourists discusses the whole question of his appearance in Englund. -

Tho following extr,i—et from the letter will, no doubt, be read with interest :

THE FRAWLEY PROBLEM. MOSE Northern Union football followers are aware that there is likely to be trouble over the playing of Dan Frawley, the speediest wing three- quarter back in Australia. Before Frawley was selected to accompany the team there were whisperings of shoals ahead, and correspondence passed frequently between the English and the New South Wales bodies. The Englith side were staunch in the attitude that Frawley would he unable to play for any team with the exception of Warrington. But the New South Wales body must have been just as satisfied that he could turn out, otherwise Frawley would never have been sent 13,000 miles on a wildgoose chase. From what Frawley nape, it would appear that in 1909 he returned to England from Australia, and signed on with the Warrington club for one season only. When the contract was fulfilled—at least Friwley thought it was—he went back to Australia and played for his old club, Eastern Suburbs. The Warrington club " kicked up a dust" and contended that Frawley was their men until they transferred -him. On the other hand, Frawley declared that his contract with Warrington had expired, and he was free to play wherever he wished. THE COLONIAL OPINION. From what one could gather from English files and letters received at Naples, Frawley is still the slave" of the Warrington club, and will be until he iv" sold "to some other master. This traffick- ing in players is the one deplorable thing in con- nection with professional football. It will never gain a hold in Australia. where what is known as -the "electoral "system is in vogue. Certain defined boundaries are set apart, and a man to play for that district must have a residential qualification of six months. But if a person comes from another -State or country, a few weeks will mike him eligible for the club be desires to join. Frawley's case is peculiar, and it is hoped will be smoothed over as quickly as possible. It would indeed, be unfortunate if the controlling bodies were to get at loggerheads before the tour began. Frawley states that when he signed on with Warring on the New South Wales Lsague asked for a transference fee of £25, which the Warrington club had never paid. It does seem hard to under- stand thatibecause a rnan contracts to play with a club for one season hia football career should be always controllef by that particular body until such time as it " sells " him to another club. Surely if a man signing for the period Frawley evidently did wished to change his allegiance he should not be hindered. The matter is certain to be threshed out as soon as the Australians reach England. Frawley would be a big ions to the visiting team which has fifteen forwards and only thirteen backs with it. There is no question about tha keenness of the -tourists to play Frawley, whom they consider their beat three-quarter now that Messenger is out of the ranks. The Warrington man has improved consider- ably since be sea here, and played brilliant football during the season just ended in Australia, and one can sympathise with the Australians in wishing to retain his services for the tour in England. But rules are ru'es, and there is no question that Frawley is Warrington's player until he is transferred or given permissicn to play with the Colonial team.

The Colon'al authorities were informed by the N.C. before their team was picked that whilm in Eagland Frawley could play for Do one but the War- rington Club, so that they took the risk with their •eyes open in bringing him over. The letter above practically admits this, and I can only presume that the Colonial managers are hoping to conduct negotia- tions on the spot which may be fruitful. If they can- not spme Frawley, neither can Warrington, ) were quite willing t t bring Frawley over themselves and sign him on again for this season. It has been suggested that a compromise might be affected by which Frawley played for Warrington and was released for the Colonial " test" matches, of which there are three. There is something to be said for the suggestion, if it is workable under the rules, and agreeable to the parties concerned. Warrington could easily spare the player for a few of ,he tourists' big games, as the tour concludes on January 13th, when there is still nearly half the

===== 0002601_19110916_art0021.txt | included=true | included =====
season

There is one statement in the letter above which is untrue, and unfair to the Warrington Club i.e. the statement that Warrington never paid the £25 transfer fee to the New South Wales League. As a matter of fact Warrington paid 3:50 for Frawley and O'Malley in December, 1900 the money being handed over to the Northern Union for the N.S.W. League.

And now for more local matters. Warrington -failed at St. Helens pretty badly on Saturday. True, they were only beaten by ten points to two and ought to have scored more, but they never looked like winning. St. Helens were not much superior in skill, hut they had a younger and more energetic team and quite overplayed the Wirepullers,particulariy forward. Warrington had a big advantage in weight, but too many of the men have lost the. enthusiasm and fire which they posaessed in youth. This early defeat went to show that the policy of building up a vounger and faster team must be steadily persevered with.

There was a surprising lot of excitement at Knowsley-road. The home players were determined to win, and they went into the struggle with so much vim that Warrington were on the defensive for the greater part of the game. Tilley was great in the rear, and fielded and punted in fine style, but when it came to running he, of course, was a spectator. Llayd had to work the pack for Creevey, and was a failure : indeed it was surprising that the ex-St. Helens man played so well considering that he was well watched and that he missed the robust play of Brooks at the _base of the scrum. Ile three 9narter line was exceedingly weak as an attacking force largely because Dickenson had an off-day and his want of speed and dash were more marked than usual. Unsworth did pretty well, but Woodburn and Brooks were both starved and "marked." In fact the latter was marked with a beautiful black eye before the finish. Some of the Sainte were anything but gentle in their methods, and ne dto look out for squalls when they come to Wildman° The referee, Hr. Whiteley, was also anything but a brilliant success.

-- To-day the Wirepullers are at home to Hull, and should record a victory, if current form and past history are anything to go by. Hull lost both their opening matches, and the defeat at home by Halifax la,t Saturday has made desperate men of them. They will try hard for victory to-day, and the Warrinaton men will need to improve vastly on the display of last iisturday if they are to capture the spoils.

It was hoped that One or two changes could have been made ut the team, but injuries and other causes compel the Committee to depend on practically the 441110 thirteen for the present. Waywell's foot Is not quite tight, and the new men are still beibg angled for.

Warrington Fall at St. Helens.

There were —hopes, locally, that Warrington would repeat their victory of last season on the ground of the Saints. but Saturday's match proved a disappoint- ment. Warrington were supported by about 1,000 followers, and an enthusiastic crowd of 8,003 lined the rails. St. Heleos were fully represented, but Warring- ton bad to substitute A. Boardman for Skelhorn in the pack, while the absence of Bradshaw re.ulted in Lloyd coming in at half aol Brooks going on the wing. The team could hardly be cousidared any weaker by the exchanges, and ought to have made a better show in the scoring than it did. The weather was again very hot, but both teams stayed the pace wonderfully welL The teams and officials were:—

St Helens—Turall, back ; Barton, J Greenwood, Molyneux and Flanagan, three-quartet backs ; Scholes, and Trenwitb, half becks; Ben Briers, Prescott, Mavitty, Pops, Les and Butterfield, forwards. Warrington—Tilley, back ; Btooks, Dickinson, Una worth and Woodburn, three-quarter backs ; " Matt " Creevey and Lloyd, half backs ; Cheaters, Johnson, Thomas, Shugars, A Boardman and Naylor, forwards.

Referee, Mr .7 W Whiteley, Halifax. Touch judges, Messrs H Naylor, Wigan, and R Acton, Widnes. The Saints were early on the aggressive but a free kick drove them back, and the Warrington forwards taking up the running soon had Turtill in difficulties. Creevey tried a short kick and followed up, but the salad carried the ball too far. Warrington maintained an aggressive position bat the combination NVB3 poor and St. Helens gradu illy forced the game at the other end. Creevey again got the ball away, and it travelled across to Woodburn who was well watched by Flanagan. Dickinson and Brooks both made efforts to get clear which were characterised more by vigour than skill, and a smart rush by the ;home forwards nearly settled the Wirepuller? aspirations to open the scoring. Prescott and Briers raced down the field together, end the latter looked all over a scorer, when Matt Creevey rushed across and saved. Then Greenwood got away and passed to Lee, who was forced to yield to superior numbers. However, an interesting movement in which Flanagan, Treowith, Briers, Pope, and Lee took part, and in which all were more or less injured, resulted in Lee opening the score for St. Helot:6,Bnd Turtill kicked the goal. Some flue exchange kicking left the advan- tage with Warringtor, but not for long, as Greenwood and !Wavily got away with Flanagan in attendance. ralavity was brought down, but when the ball came loose Flanagan snatched it up and scored. Faulty play by Alta Creevey and Brooks rasultsd in a good chance not being utilised, but just before the interval Thomas kicked a penalty goal for Warrington. Half- time: St. Helens, S points; Warrington, 2 points. , When the second half opened, the pace was not by any means as fast as it had been in the first half, in which the players had had a lot of gruelling. For a time the play was somewhat tame, and then a round of passing enabled the St. Helens backs to reach the Warrington quarter, but there was a quick change when Uusworth broke away, Red when in a good position he pawed to Diokioson, who must have been grievously disappointed when he knocked on with the line at his mercy. Tilley failed to play the ball after being tackled and this enabled Turtill to add another goal to the St. Helens score. Lloyd and Brooks changed places but the Warring'on men could not get going, though the forwards shaped pretty well. Tilley bad a very busy time facing the wind, but he defended finely. Os one occasion the ball was sent past him by Molyneux, but Unsworth came to the rescue and kicked de id. Near the close both teams came near scoring again. Prescott being tackled close in. Warrington had the worst of the game to the end. Final : St. Helens, 2 goals 2 tries (10 points) ; Warrington, 1 goal (2 paints).

NORTHERN UNION MEETING. New standing Order.

At a n.ecting of the fletTeral Committee of the Northern Rugby Union, held at Iluddertfield on Tues- day night, a new standing order was made as follows : —.Chat the rules of all junior and intermediate lesgue, and competitions uner the authority of the Northern Union are accepted only on the cvnditions that clubs and pla3 era have the right to appeal to the respective county cAtmittees culls deposit of ono guinea, such appeal to be made within fourteen days of the decision of the League or Competition Committee against which the appeal is being made." '111101dt:8m Club asked for a ruling on the following question A defending side are penalised ten yards from their goal line. Tie attackirg aide take the kick at goal. Ihe bell strikes one of the defending players (who is standing on the charging line with his back to the kicker) and goes over the nvad WI lire. Should a dead ball be allowed, or should the ball be brought back and scrummaged where it touched the defending pla3er ? " Answer : The tall should be brought tack and scrummaged. The arrangements for the reception of the Australian team have been considerably modified. The team is expected to arrive at Plymouth this Friday, and they mill afterwards proceed by train to London. It is yet uncertain whether the banquet in London' given bv the brother of the president of the New South Wales League, will he on Saturday or Monday next. The Australians' executive purpose after the banquet to send up the selected team to Coventry to train there during the week, arid the remainder of the party will go direct to their headquarters, Gordon Lodge, Southpolt.

FR 4WLEY'S POSITION.

The Australian League cable the Northern Union to ask if D. Frawley. the ex-Warrington three-quarter back woula be allowed to play for the "Kangaroos" if selected. As this is a distinct breach of the transfer laws and the implied agreement the Northern Union cabled back a refusal.

FRAWLEY THE FASTEST MAN.

..... Frawley is the fastest man in the present team. He is brilliant on attack and his previous experience on English grounds will baa great advantage. He won the 100 yards championship of the Kangaroos and beat everybody who challenged him in the Eastern Suburbs Championship. He is even faster on the field than on the track and he handles the ball well.

SEND OFF TO I'RAWLEY.

Dan Frawley, the League selected Eastern three- quarter on tour, prior t, his departure for England, was the recipient of a handsome travelling rug, the gift of his fellow workers at Moin and Co's, hide and skin store, Kent-street.—The storeman, Mr. Franbarg, in handing the presentation, remarked that he hoped it would keep Dan's hide warm like the fur does ton winter bunny in his furrow, and that he would return safe and sound to the Land of the Golden Fleece with the same reputation as he leaves it, the bounding kangaroo ; and so say all of us. From the Austral:an "San."

CRICKET AND BOWLING.

Cricket Notes. The Warrington club brought the season to a close a somewhat inglorious fashion on Saturday,

lewton-le-Willows 'defeating them on their otirt ground, with five wickets in hand. It is only fair to point out that the home eleven were without the services of their professional, whilst the bowling *as farther weakened by the absence of W Bennett. Going to the wicket first the Warrington men were all dismissed for 110—a poor score on a good pitch. J. Callon made the beat individual display, his contribution being 28. Newton made a poor start, three wickets having fallen for 31, but T. Marsh and W. Dudgeon placed a different complexion on the game, the score having risen to 78 before Dudgeon was out leg before. Marsh distinguished himself with a fine not out innipga of 52.

Grappenhall achieved a creditable victory at the expense of Runcorn " A," the respective tot4ls being 72 and 3L G. Toulmin batted well frr 25 for the victors audit's° took eight wickets.

Bowling Notes. WARRINGTON AND DISTRICT BOWLING

ASSOCIATION. 13CLL's HEAD V. NORTH ASHTON FINAL FOR THE CHAMPIONSHIP.

Under the new iule rf home and home gimes, the North Ashton hayinw pick d greens,dtcided to play at the Bull's Head first. Last Saturday brought together an excellent crowd. The green, which has had every attention, was in splendid order. Bull's Head con- quered last year's winners by 53 points. I hardly consider the total large enough to carry to North Ashton, but one cannot always tell.

A Gleave sot the ball rolling by winning 21-13 from J E Welsby ; 8 points to the good was business for the Bull's Head.

Jack Swanton did equely as well in capturing G poictr for his team, W Shaw found the green very difficult.

E Shaw defeated NV Ir-rwe 21-16, bat Lows must he complimented on the excellent game he played. He was sadly in arrears when the game registered 16-8 in Sharree favour, and only to be defeated by 5 points Fpeaks well.

Joe bi leave and George Davies gave a good display of bowling. It took 32 ends to complete the game, peeling constantly ; the game was cried 20 all. With a desperate finish Weave came along and drew the final end. Both players deserved the hearty cheering which greeted them.

Andrews cut up very badly against Ted liaselden. It was surprising to find Haselden always there on such a tricky green. It was good bowling that beat Andrews.

Tom Rigby proved his worth on the Bulls Head team in defeating W. Nekton by 21-11. --- Henry Eyes had a runaway game against F. Cart- wright winning quite easily 2-ft.

It Ins been arranged to play the return game at North Ashton on Septemler 30th Int.

Tom Ellison officiated at the dust guns last Eaturday.

OLIVE BRANCH LODGE TOURNAMENT. Favourerl with delightful weather, the members of the Olive Branch Lodge of Oddfellows, had a most enjoyable day last Satinday, when they met in their annual tournament at Ilalton Castle. The green wdll excellent condition, teenty-two members took part. There was a decided improvement in the bawling all round, compared with previous years. The final for the competition was between T P For aes with three start. with H Walker on scratch. It was a typical filed P Forbes winning 13-10, H Walker failing La suaceed by giving three start.

The Consolation Final brought together G. Savage and H. Bently with starts of lire and six respectively. Savage looked always • winner pia, ing excellent all through and ran out an easy winner 12-11, never allowing Bentley to register a single chalk. The mem- , bers then adjourned for tee which was splendidly served by the proprietor at Halton Castle Hotel. After tea a smoking concert and distribution of pri,, took place, presided over by NG. George Ilrown. Before the proceedings opened the chairman referral to Bro. P. G. Taylor, who had heel taken from then. with tragic suddenness, and their deepest sympathy went out to their a orthy Sacretary and family in thsir sad bereavement. The wLole of the members standing a resolution of sympothy was pulsed in silence.

THE EXAMINER.

SA ITRDAY, SEPTEIIBER 16, 1911

- lie Eccr cmiit," in A REVOLT leading article on the AGAINST Morocco question, WAR. emphasizes the truth

of the statement that there is no quarrel between the peoples of Great Britain and Germany. "The quarrel that has been coming on so long is a quarrel of diplomatists and newspaper writers," and does not represent the conviction or sentiment of the democracy either here or in Germany. Feeling in the German capital is utterly opposed to war, and a meeting there, called by the Chauvinists to stir up feeling on Morocco, was a failure. Oa the other hand "the Conservative meeting was followed on Sunday by a monster gathering of the working-classes, estimated variously at from one hundred thousand to three hundred thousand. The "Berliner Tagblatt," which ought to know, estimates the con- couree at half-a-million. This monster gathering, as the " Daily Chronicle" informs us, was summoned by the" Vor- warts," 'the organ of the Social Democrats, in the following words : People of Berlin ! Turn out in your greatest numbers at Sunday's Demonstration. Proclaim your unshakable determination for peace ! Morocco is not worth the bones of a single German workman. Down with the war fever ! Long live solidarity with our English ani French brothers.

Truly an immense demonstration, and it passed unanimously peace resolutions of the most uncompromising character. But had meeting one-tenth the size been held in favour of war, there would have been columns of descriptive and exaggerated com- ment in the London Press. In fact it was hardly noticed, and yet the German Social Democrats,who correspond with our Radicals and Free-traders, are already so powerful that the German Government fears they may win a conclusive victory in the next Reichstag elections. We will not say that such a meeting would be possible in London or any of our large towns, for the possibilities and meaning of a great war are not comprehended, and the popular mind is still taken up with the railway strike and the labour unrest. But we do say that a policy of unfriendliness towards Germany has no popular or com- mercial support in England. We have an enormous and profitable trade with Ger- many, whose suspension would probably throw 10 per cent, of our working-classes out of employment. And, moreover, we are bound to the people of Germany by ties of blood, by historical traditions, by the debts which we owe German philosophy, German ' iterature, and German science. The Ger- mans recognise a corresponding debt, and ' most of the political reforms of Germany have been undertaken under the influence and by the light of our own free institutions Nor should ItThe forgotten that many of our own social projects, such as old age pensions, have been guided by German experience."

When Aldermah AN ALDERMANIC Pierpoint's name was COMEDY. struck off the burgess

roll on Saturday by the Revising Barrister, many people jumped to the conclusion that there would be an Alder- manic vacancy in November. Our contem- porary on Wednesday announced Alderman Pierpoint's retirement and discussed the vacancy which was to ensue. Other people specially interested in the matter have been even more premature in their judgment, and we believe that the Tory party has already —in imagination—filled the seat which will not be vacant and to which they have no claim if it was relinquished. Alderman Pierpoint has sent a letter, which we print in another column, announcing that he has no intention of retiring, and basing his decision on the precedent set by the late Alderman Pickmere many years ago. He is disqualified to act as an Alderman, but he can hold the seat for the remainder of the period for which he was elected—which happens to be five years from November next—so that there is plenty of time for the Alderman to renew his qualification as a burgess of the Borough. We are glad that Alderman Pierpoint has decided to "sit tight," for many reasons. With the exception of Alderman Bolton he is senior member of the Council; he is held in great respect by the representatives of all parties, and his resignation would, we fear, have been followed by an unseemly political scramble for the vacant seat.

I gave some news the COMPULSORY other day (says the SERVICE IN London correspondent AUSTRALASIA. of the "Manchester Guardian ") of the movement that is being organised here to agitate for compulsory military service for boys on the basis of the new Australian com- pulsory service. Some interesting details now reach me about the working of the service in Australia and New Zealand. It

appears that of the 70,000 boys of eligible age in New Zealand some 13,000 have not regis- tered. The proportion is expected to be somewhat similar in Australia,though figures are not available. In New Zealand prosecu- tions have been instituted in the ordinary magistrate's court against those who had omitted to register at the beginning of July. Pleas of conscience have been of no avail; fines have been imposed, or in default in several cases twenty-one days' imprisonment. In the case of Peter Thompson, heard at Nelson on July 22, the defendant was told he must choose between the instruc- tions of his parent and the law, and the law had first claim. There is no reason to suppose this to be an isolated case. The members of the Society of Friends in both countries after careful consideration decided to register, but with the paper has been handed in a protest. In New Zealand, how- ever, there is a special "conscience clause" for boys belonging to "certain well-known I denominations, such as Quakers," in which' case the boy is to be posted to non-combatant branches without further question. In the i Australian Act there is no exemption from training on the score of conscience—only from service in time of war. Probably, how- ever, the gravest injustice of the Acts is that they disfranchise the boy-objectors of to-day when they grow up. The compulsory registration clauses are only applicable to those under 21, i.e., non-voters. Persons convicted of refusing service are, however, disfranchised, and when they reach their majority will be unable to claim the right of voting. These new regulations have given a great stimulus to the work of the different peace organisations in the Antipodes, many new societies being formed and old ones revived.

When the holiday truce AN INTERESTING in political controversy BY-ELECTION. comes to an end, the Home Rule question must inevitably come to the front. It already has come into the field at the Kilmarnock Burghs By-election. There an interesting candidature promises fair to bring a Glad- stone once more into the House of Commons. Mr. W. C. G. Gladstone not only has in his favour the sentimental recommendation of the greatest name in Liberal history, but the qualification of a large and patient study of political questions such as only an excep- tional sense of responsibility imposes on any one who feels the vocation to a political career. It is regrettable that the Labour Party should have decided to split the Pro- gressive vote in the first by-election in which the Home Rule question has been a principal issue since the passing of the Parliament Bill.

The rain-fall of -Tuesday and Tuesday night was one of the heaviest experienced in the district for some years. No less than 227 inches of rain fell, and the Appleton Reservoir rose two feet the following day. A day later, however, the rise had ceased entirely, indicating that the ground itself has taken nearly all the moisture. To authorities whose water supply is getting perilously short, the down-pour was little more than tantalising. Luckily. Warrington has abundance of water at Winwick and could probably spare a, few million gallons if there was any means of conveying it to dryer districts.

One or two Tory members are refusing to pollute their pockets with the £94 odd which represents their first quarter's salary. Some are giving the money to charity, and one Member wishes the Borough Treasurer of his constituency to accept it in relief of rates. These are the ostentatious givers, and not the cheerful givers whom the Lord loveth. Mr. Harold Smith, M.P., with really becoming modesty, has said nothing about his salary so far. We feared that he might be tempted to try and force it on the Infirmary or some other needy institution, and it is gratifying to note that our Member is learning slowly but surely that " silence is golden."

Mr. Keir Hardie has, as might be expected, gone to Kilmarnock, and, as might also be expected, he has repeated his shameless and contemptible slander on the Prime Minister : The only meaning of the Government's itate- ment to the railway directors was that, if neces- sary, in order to kev the railways running, the strikers would be shot down. Mr. Keir Hardie's conduct in the House of Commons showed him quite determined to go on with this slander. It may do some mischief, but we cannot believe that the electors of Kilmarnock will credit such rubbish. We note that "John Bull" has taken Mr. Keir Hardie to task, and while perhaps he is not the best critic and judge who might have been selected, he is doing the job very effectively. • • •

Councillor Middleton, of Oldham, took a wise course at the open-air meeting on Monday, when he put Home Rule in the forefront of the Liberal programme, and advanced some unanswerable arguments in its favour. The other side are still flogging a dead horse in Tariff Reform, and ignoring contemporary politics. One of Councillor Middleton's best points was: If Home Rule was not before the country at the last election (as Tories now assert) why did they cover the hoardings with pictures of "The Dallar Dictator," and fatuous queries such as: " Shall Britons be sold for foreign gold ? " The fact was, as the speaker pithily said, that all the Tory objections to Home Rule were before the country, but the people took no notice of them!

* The Lord Advocate at a conference in Glasgow, on Monday, moved a resolution urging the Government to hasten the completion of the land valuation and to make it publiu,,.so that the rating authorities might be granted the power of adopting the value of the land as the standard of local rating. At the same time he deprecated

hurry, and said the,valu ttion was proceeding rapidly and with frictionless ease. He rejoiced to think *hat lend taxation had ceased to be a party question. Reformers had nothing to do now but reap the fruit of their victory. * *

ICountless message; of condolence have poured in upon Mr. Ramsay Macdonald. Mrs. Macdonald was always a woman with I many friends, from the days when she was I a young Fabian conspicuous for the enthusiasm with which she flung herself into the work of research and propaganda, to those in'which, a kind-eyed matron, she played the hostess to Labour-politicians in her pleasant rooms over-looking Lincoln's Inn Fields. Within 18 months Mr. Macdonald has lost his wife, his mother, and a promising little son.

a The Prime Minister received a host of con- gratulations on Tuesday, on reaching his 59t1f birthday. There is no gainsaying the face that in the estimation of the nation Mr. Ibiquith stands higher than any one supposed he-would do when he succeeded Sir H. Campbell-Bannerman. He has made a deep mark on the political life of England, and by the aid of the Parliament Bill has opened a new chapter in the constitutional history of the country. The "Birmingham Daily Post" (the Unionist organ,so closely identified with Mr. Chamberlain) pays a remarkable tribute to his statesmanship in Monday's issue. It says, "There is nothing of the mountebank or charlatan about him. He has definite aims—be they right or wrong—an inflexible purpose, courage and decision in action. When the secret 'history of our time to light there will be two instances .I.bl baling him as a man of strength- io.itant decision to use the full forces of in.; , at whatever cost of life, to keep opon ti:b ports and railways, and the equally prompt decision to see fair play between Prime germany."

A decision given last week by the tra•lf3rd Stipendiary Magistrate (writes a Landoll correspondent) shows how even a carefully worded Act of Parliament may leave somc loopholes, and the puipose of the Act itself be thereby defeated. Hitherto it has been assumed that only bona-fide travellers could be served with intoxicating liquor in licensed premises on Sundays during prohibited hours. Now it seems, from Mr. Wilberforce's decision, th it a man who is himself a bona- fide traveller can entertain his friends during closing hours, although they them- selves live within the three-mile limit. The case arose out of a charge preferred against a small employer of labour who travelled some distance to inspect some work being done by a few local workmen. The employer had certainly travelled more than the statutory three miles, and invited his men to meet him at a certain public-house on Sunday morning, where he treated them to liquor. The magistrate held that the Publican, who was also charged with selling, was entitled to serve to the bona-fide traveller, and the latter had a perfect right to entertain his friends, though they were local residents. If this is according to the letter of the law, it is certainly in opposition to its spirit.

NOTES AND NOTIONS.

If Councillor W— R-- is biting his fingers at the unexpected denouement of the situation created by Alderman Pierpoinrs disqualification as a burgess, then surely Councillor J-- W— can afford to smile!

Councillor Pembarton is a Conservative— or half-a-one at least—yet it is rumoured that if he has an opponent in November it will be a Tory nominee. The name of a 'gentleman employed at Wilderspool brewery is mentioned. • • •

The Warrington Pitilomathic Society is evidently not going to let its reputation for enterprise suffer during the coming season. Already, so distinguished a lady as Miss Ellen Terry has been secured and will give a dramatic recital in Parr Hall on November 9th on "The Triumphant Heroines of Shakespeare." *_*_*

During the year 1910-11 no less than 1,325 Warrington girls received instruction at the icookery centre, and 1,222 earned the Board of Education's grant. As practice assuredly makes perfect we hope these young ladies will be assiduous in culinary experiments at home. Paterfamilias must swallow his prejudices together with the new dishes, and think only of the rising generation. The" Die-hards" have formed themselves into a new party" auxiliary to the present Conservative forces," with Lord Halsbury as their president. The "Daily Chronicle" suggests that they should call themselves "The Antique Bantams" in honour of the President and adopt as a watchword "B. M. G."—or" Balfour Must Go." Is the member for Warrington one of the bantams? • 4, • Mr. Furness's Tariff Reform resolution having wrecked the Conservative Govern- ment in the Parliamentary Debating Society, the Liberal party takes office in the Session which opens on October 3rd. On that date the King's Speech will be introduced, and Sir Edward Russell will be the guest of the evening. He has promised to lead off with a speech of modest length, but which will doubtless be of great interest to the members.

Before the much-welcomed rainfall of last Tuesday the Water Committee had been experiencing great difficulty in supplying water for manufacturing purposes from the Appleton reservoir. It was their intention to run off the water, as far as possible, and, after closing the Appleton mains, to supply manufacturers with Winwick water. Fortunately this may not now be necessary as the heavy rain fall at the beginning of the week raise 3 the water in the reservoir 2 feet.

Mr. C. E. Parker yesterday took to the Museum two curious specimens of fungi which he had found growing amongst the potatoes in his vegetable garden at Penketh. There is, of course, nothing extraordinary in the growth itself but the dimensions and shape in this instance are somewhat unusual. The botanical name of this type Of fungi is lycoperdon giganteum, but in homely English it is known as the giant puff- ball. The specimens may be seen in the wild flower section of the Museum. * * * _ .

Is it true that at a recent caucus meeting of the Tory party, attended by Sir Gilbert Greenall, Bart., C.V.0., and Mr. Harold Smith, M.P., a resolution was come to that the party should dictate the policy of the Town Council in the future? This is what one might call "a pious resolution" which will be difficult to carry out. A great uprising of the Blues is predicted in Novem- ber. We hope they will not be too closely associated with one particular trade.

Widnes. Warrington Wesleyan Cycling Club. . _ . . .

Ifeeti; at Latchford Swing Brrdge on Saturday the 9th September, 11 members spent another of the glorious Saturday afternoons that this year has given us is the Bollin Valley at Castle Mill, near Itingway. Immediately after starting, the carelessness of a passing cyclist who swerved to his right without look- ing, caused one f the party to be thrown into the road with cut hands and torn knickers, whilst the other's machine was badly buckled. As usual it was one of the most careful of our riders who suffered. His bruises, however, did not turn him back. Three more runs will end the season, and it is very gratifying to look back on a year of so many ' runs, and so few mishaps. Our captain was also caught sapping. Having chosen the route through Bowden, he got on unfamiliar ground, and a new read, not marked on the Ordnance Map, brought as, amid a shout of laughter, to • blind end. A retreat of 134) yards, however, put us right, and a beautiful country lane finished the first half of the 30 miles at a" refreshment farm " on the to? of the bank overlooking the river. Cyclists will be glad to hear that his L ordship of Tattoo has at last recovered his equanimity to the extent of allowing all his tenants in this beautiful district to supply visitors with ref reah- meats. The moonlight ride home was by way of Ashley and Mere Corner. ,

FOLEY CHINA TEA SERVICE 3, 41 ph eSS s GARLAND, 51, Bridge at, Warringtoo.—Advt. Warrington Puddler's Suicide. . „ .

Mr. fir Brighouse held an inquiry at Burtonwood into the circumsta, ces attending the death of Thomas Burgess. aged 23, an ironworks puddler, of 33, West- street, Warringtcn. The deceased was a single man living with his parents. He should have gone to work on the 3.d, 4th, and sth Let , but did not do no, and on going to work on Wednesday he was told his place was filled up. On Thursday Jahn Riley found a cap on the bank of the St. Helens and Sankey Canal at Burtonwood with a note pinned inside. He took it to the nearest waterman, and dragging operations were, commenced, and the body of the deceased was taken from the water. The note was in the handwriting of the deceased. He said he was sorry for the trouble he was giving and asked his father and mother to bury him as cheaply as possible.—The jury returned a verdict of suicide, but what was deceased's state of mind was nct shown. Miss Marie Houghon Sings in Canterbury Cithedral. The " Kentish Gazette and Canterbury Press,' last Saturday, states that among the fins series of organ recitals at Canterbury Cathedral arranged by Dr. Cborlton Palmer, that which took place on , Wednesilsy afternoon was one of the beat. Special importance was given to this recital by the fact that Miss Marie Houghton, a talented pupil of Madame Albani, was singing. The congregation was a very large one,the seating accommodation being fully taken op. Miss Houghton has great gifts which have been fully recognized and developed by her famous tutor. La fast, expert musical critics speak of Miss Mitie Houghton as a singer very little behind Madame Albaiii —an expression of opinion which means a very great deal indeed. Even the untrained ear, which does not detect land appreciate all the beauty and high quality of such a splendid voice was quite enraptured by Miss Houghton's singing. The quality of her voice was particularly borne in upon the listener in the opening passage of Handel', air " Rejoice greatly " ; and its great compass and the trueness of her notes were evident throughout, both in her first performance and in her expressive rendering of "My Redeemer and my Lord.'' It is said that Miss Houghton possesses rrobably the best female voice that has been heard in the Cathedral for many years. Ile remainder of the progtamme was also of a high standard. Mien Marie Houghton is engaged to sing in " Elijah," at the Albert Hall, for the Royal Choral Society.

P.& K. Moulders: Outing.

On pleasure bent, in ideal weather a party of forty- four of the moulders from Pearson A: Knowles' wJrks set out for Gatacre on Saturday by the 1.27 t,ain. Their destination was the Coffee House Hotel, Woolton iwhich they reached soon after two o'clock. Toe first procedure was to face the camera, a photograph being taken by Mr. Downie a member of the party. A game of bowls next took place, the twenty-two oldest playing the twenty-two youngest menfbers. The match proved very interesting and enthasiastie. the oldest side winning by the narrow margin of nine points. The visitors then adjourned to the dining- room where an excellent kni'e and fork tea was served, all praise being due to the host and hostess. They then separated for a short time, e rue to ',owl, others to take a walk in tbe beautiful country, returning to spend the evening at a smoking concert. Songs were rendered by Meters. Rowland, Suttee, Dodd, Cooper, Lawton, Ball and Lowe. The humorous portion of the pro- gramme was in the hands of Mr. Heaton who kept the company alive with his laughing and other comic songs, with his own banjo accompaniment. Mr. Tilling proposed a vote of thanks to Mr. Ball who had the management of the pie-sic in hand, 1r the able maneer in which he had carried the proceedings to such a successful issue. Hr. Davies seconded and the vote was warmly approved. Mr. Ball in respond- ing said he was glad that the pic-nie bad proved such a sitcoms and hoped it would be the forerunner of an annual outing. Applause.) Mr. Dooley, the pianist., and the other artistes were also heartily thanked. The party journeyed to Warri ngton by the 0.42 train, having•pent a very pleasant and enjoyable hall-day.

Fire at Bridge-foot. About 5 o'clock on Friday morning lot rmation was received at the Fire Station that a fire had broken out at the boot abd shoe shop of Mr. Walter 11080. bottom, which is the middle shop of a row of wooden buildings at the junction of Wilderspo)l-raad and Knutsford-road. WI en the brigade under Deputy- Superintendent kTurner arrived on the scene it was found that owing to the intbmimable nature of the stock the place was well alight, the flames shooting out to a considerable distance and threatening the adjoining shops. There was a good supply of water and the brigade set to work to prevent the spreid of the fire, as it was seen there was no possible chance of saving the contents of this shop. Lb,fortunately Mr. Rosehottom's premises were not insured and, as the shop and stock were com- pletely destroyed, he suffers heavy 1039. When Mr. Rosebottom arrived and saw the extent of the damage he was completely overcome and had to be assisted into a neighbour's howls. The origin of the fire is a mystery as the shop is a lock-up establishment and everything was to perfect order when Mr. Itoeebottom closed it the previous night at eleven o'clock.

A few soiled BABY CARRIAGES LO be sold 0 eq.'". Apply J. S. GARLAND, 51, Bridge IL, Warrington :Advt.

The Pelee it 1111 k. The members of the Warrington Branch 4 the Cheshire Milk Producers' Ass wiation held a meeting at the Blue Bell Inn on Saturday eve nog. ti de•ide the wholesale price of milk for the winter month.. Mr. E. Botch presided.—The position of the farmers was fully considered, and it was strongly felt that the problem of producing milk hr the next six or seven month-, was a very serious one. The pastures, it was stated, are bare and brown, with very little prospect lof improvement for swne time. In addition to this. the water supply on INDS farms is causing grave anxiety. The hay harvest generally has been the lightest experienced for 40 years, while the straw crops have been unsatisfactory. Root crops are in many cases a total failure, and even where that is not the case, the outlook is indeed glo,iny. To meet this serious condition of things farmers for the las two months have been spending, large sums of money in artificial feeding stuffs, which have steadily increased in price and are still going up. Tail) heavy expenditure will have to continue all through the winter, and even then it will tax the ability of the best men to keep the dairy stork in fit condition. There has been • falling off in the output of milk as compared with other years of from 2:', per cent to 30 per cent, and it was generally felt that to meet the present situation it was imperative that the price ehould be increased to the retailers, and through tikes to the public, the opinion being expressed that if only the consurners could realize the position in all , its seriousness, lboy would not hesitate to pay cheer- , fully a price sufficient to make the Seines profitable , for those engaged in the industry. The following , resolution was unanimously adopted : '• That from October let to April 30th, I'./12, the wholesale price , of milk be 2s. 9.1 per &stn."

A case of considerable local intereat was beard at the Borough Court on Wednesday before Mr. Charles • White (in the chair). Alderman Smet hurst, Councillor •tfackson Holmes and Mr. J. W. Hayward, sylvan George Wilson of no settled abode, was charged that being a cdapict on licence, he was found in 123 lands-street under circumstances as to satisfy the Court that he was about to committ ILD offence.—lle pleaded guilty. Chief Inspector Williamson said that in April, 1909, 'priaoner was sentenced to three yeas penal servitude at Liverpool Quarter Sessions on a charge of house- breaking but was released on licence ten months before the expiration of that term. With regard to the pre- sent charge it appeared that a man named Murphy who carried on the business of a small paper and tobacco shop in Itylandk-street made the shop secure on retiring on the night of August 11th. Next morning it was found that the lock had been wrenched off and a quantity of the wood splintered as if by some instrut mint. Early on the morning of Angina 12th, Mr. Sidney Hart, a bill post inspector, was in Rylands-street and noticed the prisoner standing in the doorway of the above shop. The prisoner looked startled when the witness appeared and put his hands behind his back as if to conceal something. Prisoner then went away but when Mr. Hart returned shortly after- wards the prisoner was again in the doorway. Mr. Hart then lost sight of the prisoner and gave informa- tion to the police. Nothing was heard of the prisoner tram that time until the 51,h trait, when information was conveyed to the police that be was again in the town. Detective Holland and Constable Summers apprehended the prisoner in a lodging-house at Friars- green, and he was conveyed to the Police Station. He was asked how long it was since he was lost in War- rington and he said nine years. When &larger' with attempting shop-breaking he replied" Do yt tr think a man of my experience would try to break in a shop like that when all 1 would get would be a few packets of cigarettes and a few coppers." Prisoner stated that he was in Birmingham on the day in question but Chief Inspector Williamson said that there were witnesses to prove tint he was in town OD that date. Yrisoner : "In it likely that 1 would go and stand in front of a shop where everybody could see me ? You fell on me Martine I have ants stain on my character. I pleaded guilty because I didn't want to go for trial as I base ten months out of the three years to serve

yet." It was stated that there was a sum of Lit in prisoner's possessisn when arrested. Detective Sergeant Howell, of the Liverpool police, said that he had known the prisoner for many year. He was undoubtedly an habitual crilinNl. When out of prison be wets continually breaking into shops by knocking otf the locks. Prisoner was sentenced to a year's hard labour, to include the ten unexpired months of the previous sentence.

Daresbury Petty Sessions.

The above Sessions were held at Deresbury cn Tuesday before Mr II Talbot (in the chair), Messrs G Dalin, Roger C Parr, H Roberta and T Baxter. POACLUNG AFFRAY AT WALTON PARE.—Richard Hughes, 82, Plumpton-street, and Joseph Bibby, 64, Back Dallam-lane, were charged with being found on the enclosed premises of Walton Park, in the ownership of Sir Gilbert Greenell, Bart., for the unlawful purpose of night poaching on August Zrd. Both pleaded guilty. Mr. Amos prosecuted and Mr. Steel appeared for the prisoner Bibby. Mr. Amos eaid that the charge which was brought under the Night Poaching Act was of a more serious nature than the ordinary prosecution for I game trespass. The prisoners, however, had been remanded from an occasional court and had been in prison about three weeks. Whilst the head gamekeeper was walking along the canal near to the park be heard a rabbit scream. He went into the park with another of the keepers and there found the two prisoners and another man, the latter however got away. The two prisoners offered not the slightest resistance. On the spot where Hogbes was arrested Was found three nets, 10 pegs and s's rabbits—Mr. Steel said that with regard to Bibby that was his lint adventure as a poacher. He was employed in Warring- ton as a hawker and had been engaged by one men for nearly 30 years. It was the prisoner Hughes that had persuaded Bibby to go on that expedition or he would never have undertaken it. Bibby was a married man and during the time he hod been in prison the family had had to depend upon friends. Having regard to the fact that that was his first offence and that he had been in prison for three weeks, he hoped the Bench would take a lenient view of the cone upon an undertaking from Bibby that he would never repeat the offence. The Bench sentenced Hughes to one month's imprison- ment and Bibby to one day. Mr. Amos asked that the articles found should be forfeited and this was granted. HORSE ILL-TREATMENT. - John Henry Devonport, Shovel and Broom Farm, Appleton, was charged with ill-treating a horse by working it in an unfit state on August 25th, at Stockton Heath.—A constable said that on the day in question he saw the defendant in charge of a horse attached to a spring cart in London-road. lie noticed that the horse was rather uneasy sake examined it and found an old large wound on the near shoulder which had smeared over with grease. Inspector Deep of the R S.P.C.A. corroborated the statement about the wound and stated that it must have been there for a considerable time. Defendant stated that the wound wan caused by a wasp sting. He was obliged to go into Warrington for some things and no , had to take the horse. A fine of 10s. including costs was imposed. Church Box Broken Into.

A box in St. Pal' s Church was broken open by a thief a few days ago and the cash contents abstracted. The exact amount, however, is not known, though it is believed that one or two pew rents had been placed in the box.

THE SHOOTING TRAGEDY.

HOW MR. F. G. TAYLOR MET HIS DEATH.

" A Pure Accident."

" A. pure accident" was the phrase us,d by the West Denbigshire Coroner (Mr. Robert Davies), in describing the tragic circumstances under which Mr. Frederick George Taylor, aged 3, eolicitor, Warring- ton, met his death whilst out with a shooting party at Cern, Lear Denbigh, on Ftiday of last week. At the inquest on Saturday the full details of the lamentable occurence. were given._

Henry Taylor, 1, Bereeford-street, Warrington, a brother of the deceased, gave evidence of identifica- tion. He added that when he last saw his brother alive ten days previously, deceased was in his usual good health. Philip Carlyle Garnett, of Penketh, near Warrington, with whom deceased had been spending a holiday at Cefn Main Farm, spoke of the slacoting party at Plas Meifod which deceased and he accompanied cn the day of the tragedy. There were five guns and four beaters, deceased acting as one of the beaters. Mr. Taylor never carried a gun. They started about ten o'clock, and at the time of the accident they were in a turnip field. The party was split up and three nuns and three beaters net off along the turnips. Witness, Mr. Posnett and the deceased remained behind. Witness and Mr. Taylcr were sitting on the hedge bank, and Mr. Posnett wood up. The latter expressed his intention of g dog with the three guns, which were n t above 70yarcla oftand asked them to take care of his coat till he came back. He believed that Mr. Posnett hit coat off whilst he had the gun in his hand. Witness was not actually watching and the gun and &tat might have been on the side of the hedge at the time. He did not see the position of the gun when it went off. He immediately attended to the deceatml, who had rolled over, but was still on the hedge bank. Deceased dil not utter any sound. He found that Mr. Taylor had been very badly shot on the left side of the head, above the ear. Death was instantaneous. He could not say how the gun went off, or explain how the accident happened, but in his opinion the affair was a pure accident. Robert Harold Ps nett, leather manufacturer, Runcorn, stated that at the time or the occurrence deceased was lying on the bank, whilst witness stood facing him. Whi!st he was in the act of giving the deceased his coat the gun was under his right arm, pointing to the ground. Witneas was standing close to the feet of the deceased and about two yards from his head. His coat was in his left band, ends, he was transferring it to the deceased the latter partly rose when taking the coat froin him. Deceased reached out with his left hand for the coat, and the gua went off when he was in the act of taking it. He conld not explain bow it went off, but certeinly he had not got his hand on the trigger. The gun was on " safe " when on the bank, but when he was ready to start it might have been at full cock. It was the back trigger that went off and possibly a button caught in between the triggers. Witness exercised every care and the whole thing was a pure accident. He desired ta express his deep regret at the distressing occurrence. Dr. Jackson, Grove-place, Denbigh, said the injuries sustained were to the whole portion of the half of the left side of the vault of the skull, and death must have been inst cutaneous. _

000.0 .0.0.0. 00.0 The Corouer said that from the evidence it was quite clear that the affair was a pure accident and not due to any carelessness. It must have been a matter of seri'ma trouble to Mr. Povnett.

The jury returned a vordict of " Accidental death " and the Coroner joined with them in an expression of deep sympathy sith the family and friends of the deceased.

THE FUNERAL

A touching simplicity characterised the funeral of the late Mr. Taylor which took place at the Warring- ton Cemetery at noon on Monday. It was the wish of the family that the ceremony should be as quiet as possible, but so many townspeople were desirous of paying a last tribute of respect to one who bad been ,so actively ideutified wi h good works, that the attendance was a very large and representative one. There were no carriages, the mourners walking from the house to the Cemetery, which it only a short distance away. Following the relatives came a long procession, including numerous members if the legal profession, friendly society °a:leis and deputations from various organizations with which the deceased bad been associated. At the Cemetery gates the cortege was met by the Rector (Rev. Canon Willis) and the Rev. T. Vickers who conducted the last riots in the chapel and at the graveside. The coffin bwe the simple inscription " Frederick George Taylor, died September 6th, 1911 ; aged 38 years." Oa it was placed one beautiful wreath, "With tenderest remembrance from Mr. and Mrs. Robert Posnett.".

The services at the Parish Church on Sunday were of a memorial character, for Mr. F. G. Taylor was one of the most active and highly respected officer, of the church. In the morning the hymns included " Let saints on earth in concert sing with those whose rest is won " acd " Light's abode, celestial Salem "and at the close of the service the congregation stood while the organist, Mr. F. M. Darby. MII3. Bac., gave an impressive rendering of the Dead Mirch in "Saul.' At evensong the choir sang with much feeling the anthem " The souls of the righteous are in the bands of God "and the congregation joined in the solemn hymn" Brief life is here our portion." The RD:79R preached from the words "And the King was much moved, an.l went up to the chamber over the gate, and wept ; and as he went thus he said '0 my son, Absalom, my son, my son, Absalom ! Would God I had died for thee, 0 Absalom, my son, my son !" (11. Samuel, :via., 33,) Canon Willis dwelt upon the untimely and unnatural death of Absalom, who was shout 30 years of age, and might have counted upon along life, but he came to ' an untimely and unnatural mid. The grief of David was intense. The death of their young friend and church officer, Mr. Taylor, had canoed a great shock and grief to all in Warrintr.n, the circumstances

being no sad. Having referred to the tragic circum- stances, and to the fact that it was by a pure accident that the gun we, discharged, the Rector said he wanted the et ngregation to understand what kindness was shown by the neighbours, and by the Noncon- formists, who opened their chapel for the reception of the body. There were, said the Rector, three great looses. There was the great loss to the town. They thought a great deal of their able and capable men, and en important occasions and in great crises they liked to have men who could advise them how to act. They had every reason to believe that if God had spared Mr. Taylor's life he would have become a very prominent character in Warring- ton. He was much trusted and thought of by his firm, and be believed the whole town felt that is had sustained a lora. But his death was not only a loss to the town, but to the Church They always valued his opini n at their Church Council meetings and attached great weight to it, for he mass lawyer, and, though he was ac mparatively young man, his legal knowledge was often of great use to them. He was a foundation manager of one of the schools. and the congregation knew what a great interest he t oat in the p trochial sale. A large pr? portion of the profit was often devoted to parochial institutions, and it would be remembered that he took the very greate-t interest in the sales and dii every- thing he could for the good of the church and parish. Then they could all undardand what a terrible I was hit death was in his own home. He was the eldest ant. The father had leaned upon him and thought so much of him, that he (the Rector) remembered Mr. Taylor, gen., once saying to him. " Fred haa a good head on his rhouldera. " It wars a t Itching sight to see father and son ; in fact. he hardly ever went up Manchester-road during the afternoon without seeing the father with the son, ofvn taking his arm They could, therefore, well u tdersatrul that the death mass very great lons in the hone There were three lesson% which might be drawn from the accident. The first was that good came out if evil. There war nothing which drew neople together so much as a calamity. Occasions of joy drew them all together, hut not so much as sad occasions, He trusted that in this case the death, which they all deplored, would draw them all nearer to ore another. Then, secondly, David's trier was for art unworthy son, who did not deserve his father's affection, but on this occasion the regret was for a worthy eon. No acn amid have been more to his father than Mr. Taylor was, and they could, therefore, understand the iirtensity of the father's grief, but he earnestly wished ' him to realize that that grief ran lessened in accord- ance with the goodness of the departed. David said, " I shall go to him; he shall not return to me." lie could not have had any hope of meeting that son here- after unless it was God's good pleasure for him to do so. But in this case the grief was great, and the j and the hope was this, that the father and son would meet again, because the son, they had every reason to believe, was a good man. The best lessons to be learnt were Oa need of preparatima and the uncertainty of life. Who would have thought that their friend, who was in the prime of life, was going away to his death? They all expected to pee him back again in his place at church, and yet God had seen fit to take him in a momerd, without the chance of preparation, except the preparation of a good life. At the close of the service Mr. Darby played Chopin's " Much Funebre," the congregation standing.

MAGISTERIAL REFERENCE.

Prior to the business of the Court at Daresbury Petty Sessions on Tuesday sympathetic reference was made to the sudden death of Mr. Taylor the deputy clerk.

Mr. If. TALBOT, the presiding magistrate, said that it was his painful duty to refer to the sad accident which had removed from their midst one who had been of great assistance to those on that Bench and one who would be missed greatly in Warrington. Their sympathy went out to the family of the deceased, and not only to the family but to the friends of the deceased who were present when that awful accident took place. As far as they ;the magistrates) were concerned they had always found Mr. Taylor to do his work in a most business-like manner and he (Mr. Talbot) was sure that the Bench would feel his loss. He believed that the deceased had been employed by Mr. Oreenall practi- cally all his life. Mr. H. GRBENALL (the clerk) said that the deceased had been employed by the firm for upwards of 19 years. During that time he had proved not only a capable and honourable man but an extremely loyal and favourable man.

T. S. STEEL (solicitor) said that on behalf of the solicitors he would like to associate himself with the remarks concerning Mr. Taylor. The members of the profession had begun to lock upon Mr. Taylor as a man of great promise, and that personally he had lost. great friend. They were boys together and had grown up together. They often came in contact, and be had found Mr. Taylor the soul of honour and virtue. Whether in social or business matte's he had never known the deceased do either an unworthy or an unhonourable act lie (Mr. Steel) was sure that all the solicitors in Warrington wou),l desire to express sympathy with the aged father and the members of the family in their sad and very sudden bereavement. Superintendent ENNION, on behalf of the police expressed sympathy with the relatives of the deeded.

WHY HURRIED BREAKFASTS ?

A FINE FOOD FOR THE EARLY RISER

The long foodless hours in some trades need to be specially considered, and their bid effects guarded against. This is particularly trite of those who rise early and perhaps have a journey to make t. their work.

The hurried breakfast of the early worker is often the soorce of serious ill-health. The human body 01114 hsve sufficient fo-d—lack of nourishment lower,' the vitality and lays one open t the attack of disease.

The bulk of the food taken is not of great conse- quence ; what matters is the amount of real nourish- ment contained therein. Flies and Dirt.—Tottenham housewives are not e little indignint coocerning a circular which has been issued by the medical officer of health. "Flies in the house," it states, " indicate either a careless or ignorant housekeeper. or both; remember, where there is no dirt ,there are no flies." This only shows the folly of overstating your case. It would have teen nearer to the mark to have said" there are fewer flies," but to say there are no flies where there is no dirt is nonsense, and few houses in the kingdom would escape the implied censure if it were true. A more practical step in the campaign against flies has been trkeo by the Dublin health authorities who are encouraging their destrnetlon by psyhog three pence for a paper bait filled with dead like. They supply the begs which require about 12,000 flies to fill them. That would seem to be no light task to undertake for three pence, but then of course the monetary reward is not the chief consideration and seeing that there is a regular trade is dead flies for chicken food, health authorities generally might new their way to paying more, and perhaps making a profit- able municipal business of the campaign simian flies. The hobble skirt, ugly and inartistic as it was, inconveniences only its wearer, but the distended skirt inconveniences everyone In its vicinity, and there is little fear that this practicsl work-e-day generation will adopt it. THE FIRST AERIAL POST.

For the first time in this country mails were carried by aeroplane on Saturday from Hendon to Windsor. A strong north-easterly breeze made flying a risky matter, but Mr. Hansel pluckily volunteered, and leaving Hendon at 4.58 he arrived at Windsor Castle at 5.11, a speed of about 105 miles an hour. The mail-bag was handed to the Windsor Postmaster and taken by a cycle postman to the Windsor Post Office, where the letters and cards were sorted. Mr. Hamel afterwards flew back to Hendon with mails, and was carried shoulder high by his enthusiastic admirers. amid loud cheers. Our picture shows Mr. Gustave Hamel beside his machine.

A RAILLESS CAR

Various Corporations in the country have been experimentiul for some time with railless cars. In Bradford, one of the first places to adopt them, the experiments have proved so successful that the Corporation recently decided to extend the system. The cars are driven by electrical pawer, supplied by twe. overhead wires, and transmitted to the car by two trolley arms. Our picture shows a car on the Laisterdyke (Dudley Hill) route.

Under the title of "My Own Story," Signora Toselli, the former Crown Princess of Saxony, will publish this week her autobiography. It will be remembered that alter leaving the Crown Prince, she married Giron, a Belgian Tutor, and later, Signor Toselli, a Florentine Pianist. Her matrimonial affairs have occupied from time to time a prominent place in the newspapers.

The match for the Sculling Championship of England this week resulted in Ernest Barry easily defeating his challenger, Fogwell, of New South Wales, and Champion of New Zealand, and thus retaining his title as champion. This is Barry's third Championship w:n.

THE WINTER'S MILK.

Why Cheshire Farmers Want a

Shilling a Gallon.

The comic il of the Cheshire Milk Producers' Asset it- tion met at Crewe oa Monday, Mr. 11..1. Tollemache presiding. A discussion took place upon the position of the milk trade it was reported that the little rain which had fallen hal not been sullit.ient to make any appreci- able difference in the pastures. It was also stated that the turnip crops had suffered moat seriously, and 13 many cases looked like being a total failure. This meant that one of the principal foods up trt which farmers relied for producing milk would not be avail- able, and could not very well he replaced. The Council decided to lame the following report and rroominenthtion to the members of the association "Owing to the prolonged drought, which has burnt up the pastures, and also caused a great shortage in the hay, straw and root crops, the prospects of winter keep for the dairy stock are very poor. In addition to this artificial feeding stuffs have increased in price, and are still going up. In spite of the heavy increased exprenditore, there has been a falling-off in the output of milk of from 25 per cent. to 30 per cent. as compared with other years. In slew of these facts, the Council are of opinion that milk cannot be profitably produced during the coming winter at a less uholesale price than Io per imperial gallon delivered. They, therefore, recommend that for winter milk only contracts for Manchester, Liverpool, Birmingham, and other town') should he at the rate of Is. per imperial gallon." The Council also recommended prices for twelve

month,' contracts for early milk, ordinary dairies, and milk or I distani,3o by rail,

But all this is somewhat wide of the mark I bad in view at the outset.
