That is bow the modest wieners of the challenge cup and 150 bear their " blushing honours thick upon them." By-the- bye, I there fill into company with an origioal sent of local character, Joseph Stansfield by name, whose proud boast it is that he has attended no fewer than 58 out of 59 September contests in question. He told me of another celebrity in his wity whose Cahn to distinction consisted in his proved ablity to " eyt three doilies (/' muffins at a sitting "—a " doffin' of muffins" being the four which the houses ife usually puts into the oven on one cake tin, so that the champion is able to consume a d.,zen tem-cakes at a meal. " WHEN KNIGHTS WERE "-- 1 was so glad tat Watrington appreciated " When Knights wet e bold." Those numerous townsfolk who share four critic's adruitation for that clover and most atnnsing Ittree will perhaps be interested in an incident in relation thereto which came under ley own observa- tion a couple of rears ago. Having wituersel and been very favourably impressed by the play on its production in London, 1 strongly advimid a friend of mine who was about to open a theatre in his native town to secure it by all means. He decided to try it for three nights as an experiment, and to my great surprise and perplexity and the disgust of the vi•iting company ef performers, it turned out a ghastly failure from the ell-intoortant financial standpoint.

Never before or since have I felt so ashamed of my fellow-creatures as I did just then. I could hardly doubt my own judgment as to the matter, and now that it has been so unmistakably confirmed by Warrington's verdict, I marvel more than ever at the dense stupidity of the inhabitants of the cotton district referred to, with its populat:on of 30,000 or so. OUR READING.

A brother scribe whom I have known at least half my life time, put it after this fashion a while ago. It nerd not all be serious reading. Serious reading, of course, every man and woman should seek to enjoy at times, the more regularly the better, for life is enriched ia the measure of one's knowledge of the worti's great books; but there is a time also for a little wholesome modern frivolity. Therefore, of course, he hastened to point that fit tion must not be neglected. It supplies the light and colour we all need to brighten and relieve the drab- ness of everyday affairs, and wisely chosen, it has an educational and en ethical value which more serious literature provides in less easily assimilated forms. This, and much more to the like effect, sprang to my mind as I read Alderman Bennett's extraordinary •jeriech at the Town Counril meeting in moving the confirmation of the minutes of the Museum Committee,

I have never in any degree shared the perturbation of alarmiits who are for ever magnifying the undoubted fact that the great proportion of book borrowers apparently prefer fiction to more solid literature. lure's a sense in which public library statistics are quite unreliable ; I nuan one who attempts to rear upon them a superstiucture of serious argument does but build upon the sand. I have always held, iny voucher being personal experience, that young peoVe with no natural taste for reading may well begin with something light and cumparatively value- less, and so be encouraged to " deeper sink and higher rise, and to per fectioa grow " not, to be sure, that I have myself" already attained," but I may humbly c'aini to be" pressing toward the mark," and cettainly began with really trashy and worthless tic ion such as is not to be found on the shelves of the Free Library. FICTION AND POETRY.

I may not elaborate nor add lengthily to this answer to one or two of the remarks which fell from the line of Alderman Bennett the other evening, especially in view of the extreme probability that indignant and vigorous opponents of that worthy's strange doctrine will this week claim the hospitality of your correspondence columns on the same account. I recall a former magazine article by Sir Gilbert Parker OM "Fiction : its place in the national life," wherein thc writer maintains that for 150 years fiction has played a part in the " criticism of life " which is as definite, if not as profound, as that played by poetry. It reveals (as he contends) the central, moral, and intellectual attitude towards all the grave questions which make the real history of a nation and the real p-aition of the great sociological problems. He rejoices to think that both England and America have masters of fiction yet alive who hold the flag high. Fiction in the United States has represented the birth of an intellectual life among the masses. Surely in that alone is abundant justifi- cation of the reasonable attitude now assumed; and how easy it would be to multiply sound arguments. To state the irreducible minimum, one of my reverend friends—a Swedenborgian a inister by the way—insists that every man shout,. possess and read " The Thoughts of Marcus Atirelius,'". The Imitation of Christ," by Thomas "The Pilgrim's Progress," " Homer's Iliad," " Gulliver's Travels," Robinson Crusoe," " Don Quixote," and "The Arabian Nights." These eight broke should be the stock in trade of all. Without a knowledge of , thent,one could not even follow the references in ordinary cotversation Cr in the daily papers. I think it is William Jewett Tucker who puts it thus A ' boy may forget his early teachers. No boy forgets ' his first books; no boy, at least of my generation, has forgotten his " Arabian Nights," his " Robinson: Crusoe," his " Pilgrim's Progress," his Plutarch. THE UM AND SUBSTANCE. ; Hastening to a conclusion, and necessarily ' omitting a great deal that might be written in support of those who, while really unsuspected students, use the Library for recreation merely, lam fain to concede a that after all there may not actually be a wide gulf I fixed between the few who are of Alderman Bennett's

way of thinking and the many wha infer that that gentleman is either seriously mistaken or greatly mis- understood. I cordially agree, r f course, that while fiction should be a recreation of the nobleet design, it must, like all other works of art, make for beauty and for peace. Above all,it should make for character. The books for all intelligent men should include history, , both ancient and modern, and the greatest masters in poetry, science, art and religion as well as fiction. Matthew Arnold was fond of novel reading, and he once advised his daughter to keep her mind elastic by reading fiction ; " only be sure," he added. "to have always some good b ok going." By a good book he meant one worth reading seriously. BM methodical ways remind one of Lord Morley's advice :—" Try for yourselves what you can read in half an licur . . . and consider what treasures you might have laid by at the earl of the year ; mad what happiness, fortitude, and wit-dom they would have given you during all the days of your life." Alderman Bennett may any I am rapidly coming round to his view point. May I repeat that all I am protesting against is the (as I believe, wholly unwarrantable as.umptions that the figures from time to time pub- lished in public library reports are reliable data upon which to base charges of frivolity and emptiness and folly againl the present and rising generations of book borrowers. As a fellow journalist pertinently and forcefully mired a while ago.—Who buys the chnap reprints of classics with which every bookseller's shop is crowded nowadays? That they are bought and cleared away ill thousands into the horn's of the people somewhere is manifest, r the publishers would t print, nor wou'd booksellers buy them. But where are the homes in which these reprints are to be found? And an answer was speetily forthcoming. A writer in the " Book It inthly "—a bright and informing causerie on books published monthly by Messrs. Simpk in, Marshall. and Co.—declares that they are to he found in the homes of " the skilled mechanics of our large industrial centres, and the colliers of the Welsh bills." " The wide range of the reading of the mechanic and the collier," he remarks, " would probably anions people "rho have not been brought into close touch with thern. The arlisao i., ass rule, more nimble- witted, more glib of tongue, than the slow-moving collier, and he shows a surprising interest in the poetry of Keats, Shelley, Byron, Burns, and Tennyson. Blessed with a retentive memory, he quotes freely, and he is by no means devoid of critical faculty. What says Alderman Bennett to this ? Moreover, it is added, the colliers know their Mill, and have more than a nodding acquaintance with Adam Smith, Marx, and Ruskin They can also dispute with you about Bishop Butler, Law, Newman and Spencer. Huxley and Arnold are not beyond their ken. And so forth and so on. This writer may tend vivatd the other extreme of exttivagance. Ile at any rate gives the rein t fancy when he argues from particular instances to a general conclusion ; all the same, many of us are decidedly of opinion that Alderman Bennett was also grievously mistaken in the line he took last week.

p.,""-",,.."-wv•-"..--,•..„-,ww, ICHILDREN'S CORNER, Conducted by UNCLE CALEB.

KITTIKIN. WHAT KI rTIKIN SAID TO THE KITTEN IN THE

LOOKING -GLASS.

Mi-a-o-u! What sort of a kitten do you call yourself, I wonder—and where are your manners, I should like to know? Here have I been standing for a quarter of an hour, saying all the pretty things I can think of to you, and not somuch as a purr can I get in reply. It is very rude, too, to mock me in that way, and imitate everything I do. My mother has always taught me to be polite to strangers; but perhaps you haven't any mother—poor thing; and never learned any manners. It is a pity, for you are a good- looking kitten—something like me, in fact, only not so pretty. Miss Jenny, my mistress, said yesterday, that I was the prettiest kitten in the world, and of course she knows, for she goes to school and learns lesson :4 out of a book. I thought a while ago that I should like to go to school too, and learn lessons. So, one day I started to follow Miss Jenny up the lane; but a great, ugly monster of a dog barked at me, and frightened me so. I thought I would learn to read too ; and as all the reading is in books, I thought the best way would be to eat one. But before I had eaten half a leaf, Miss Jenny came in, and she took away the book and called me a naughty kitten, and mother boxed my ears and sent me to bed without any supper ; so that I decided that reading was not good for kittens. Well, you strange cat !—not a word from you yet? Come, now, do be good-natured, and come out from behind that window. Such a grand frolic as we might have together! My brother Tom was given away last week. He jumped upon the breakfast table, and upset the cream-jug all over my mistress' new dress, and she said: "That comes of having so many eats about? One of them must go to-day "—and so, Tom went. Well, I cannot waste my time here any longer, for there is nothing to be got out of you but rudeness. I shall never come to see you again. And of all the ugly, rude, dis- agreeable kittens I ever saw— There—see what you've done! You made me so angry, I've knocked over Miss Jenny's beautiful blue and gold smelling-bottle, that her grandpapa gave her on Christmas. There, it has rolled off the dressing-table and broken into bits. Oh, I'm sure I'll get no supper to-night, and oh, dear, what shall I do? 0—oh! You've knocked your bottle, too! My won't you catch it, though! THIS WEEK'S STORY. THE MAN OF MEDALS.

Braggs : "You see that chap over there?" Phaggs : "Yes." Braggs " Well, he possesses more foot • ball medals than any man in the city." Phaggs : "Great Scott! Is he an international ? But as I watched him I som cmild see Ile never would sing for you and ins Any more on the apple-tree. Never more in the morning light, Never more in the sunshine bright, Trilling hie song in gay delight.

And I'm Chinking every summer day, How never, never I can repay The little life that I took away !

PILGRIMAGES

THE HOME OF "OLD PARR."

MOST FAMOUS OF CENTENARIANS

During the first General Election of 1910 I accompanied a friend, an ardent campaigner, on a hazardous motor trip into the heart of the Peak District to fetch a voter from a lonely hill-side farm. The other side were after our man too, a circumstance which made my companion indulge in reckless driving in the dark and gave me a most uncomfortable time. We got Ithere first and pollel our man, and while fortifying ourselves for the cold journey back the conversation turned upon the advanced age of the man we had just taken to record his vote. Then as a natural sequence we talked of Old Parr and his wonderful age- record. .

One of our rivals was present and began to cast doubts upon the veracity of Old Parr's tablet and averred that at any rate Yorkshire could provide a better example of longevity in one Henry Jenkins who lived to be 169. At the time I classed this as an election" inexactitude" such as the other side always indulge in, but a friend has sent me a picture post card of Henry Jenkins, whose record he came across while cycling near Bolton-on-Swale last year. I have sinc3 in a book on centenarians read an account of a meeting between Jenkins and "Old Parr" who were contemporaries. Jenkins, it is stated, was born at Bolton-on- Swale near Richmond in 1500 and followed the employment of fishing for the most respect- able period of 140 years. When about 12 years old he was sent to Northallerton with a load of arrows for the army of the Earl of Surrey. At York Assizes in 1655 he proved an ancient road to a mill 120 years before, and died at Ellerton in December 1670 aged 169 years. Such is the record as claimed by the

village of his birth and burial and, if it be correct, he richly deserved the monument they gave him in 1743. Jenkins age appears to be as well or as _ _ . _ _

little verifielasiVat of Old Parr. Parr was , certainly as John Taylor the poet said, an "old, old, very old man." When he died, but we, practically, have only his own word for it that he was 152. But he convinced many reliable people that he was correctly stating his age and it is of no use trying to give disproof at this late day. Even the sceptics are willing to admit he probably reached his 120th birthday nnd that surely is enough for his fame.

A RUN THROUGH SHROPSHIRE.

Those who want a pleasant afternoon can spend it where this prodigy of an old man spent his lifetime, and they will find it most interesting. He was a Shropshire man this old Thomas, and they show you the house among the Breidden Hills where he spent his long tale of almost uneventful years. There is a glamour and floral luxuriance about the Brieddens which is not soon for- gotten. We left Oswestry for the Welsh- pool road passing to Four Crosses and thenee to Llandrinio. Here we made a short stay to visit the old church and shortly crossed the Severn which soon unites its waters with the Vyrnwy,atthis point the larger stream of the two. The Breidden Hill had been a conspicuous object for some miles and it was now quite near on our right, and we were within a mile or so of Rodney's Pillar, which according to the inscription thereon, was erected to Admiral Rodney, by the gentlemen of the county. As the road rises about 500 feet above the highroad, we were unable to ride, but had a pleasant walk through a lane, beautiful with July wild flowers. The foxgloves were at their best and their colour magnificent. Then we had the honeysuckle and the wild rose to cheer us at intervals, and wild strawberries for refreshment. It is a spot to encourage centenarianism and we came in sight of Parr's country quite ready to believe great things as to his longevity. Reaching the summit we turned to the left to Wollaston in order to see the tablet to Old Parr in the church. The building had been closed for repairs, and we were apprehensive . . . .

, that the tablet would have been removed, but on calling at Wollaston lodge we found that it was still ir. the church, which is shortly to have a new roof, the present one being in a dangerous condition.

===== 0002601_19110916_art0075.txt | included=true | included =====
LIVED UNDER TEN KINGS AND QUEENS.

The brass tablet hangs over the pulpit and gives details of the the reigns—ten kings and queens—in which he lived. The brass does not appear to be of ancient origin, hut prob- ably takes the place of an older one,as there are marks on the church wall where some such memorial has been previously fixed. The churchyard at Wollaston has not been used as a burial ground and it is laid out almost like a garden and looks most cherful. Adjoining the yard there is an ancient Saxon burial ground marked by a high tumulus, which, with the old trees on the top, quite overshadow the small church, and there is another tumulus not far away. PARR .8 COITAGE.

The ladies at the lodge presented us with photos of the church and tablet, also of "Old Parr's Cottage," which we now went to see. Remote from the highway, it is approached by a road that is little better than a cart track. It is timber built and thatched, but was then empty. A very old tree stands oppo- site with which some legend of Parr is con- nected, but there was no one to give us the story. The position is very fine. It is 5001 t. above sea level, faces South-west, and is sheltered comfortably by the Breidden Hill. To the front there stretches a view of the Welsh mountains, and at the back a long reach of agricultural country cheers the eye. We learned on the way back that the house had been empty for some years, and our informant told us that Parr was not the only person who had seen his 100th birthday there, for one, Mrs. Davies, had lived to be 101, and had been followed to the grave by 70 descendants.

THE GUILE OF OLD THOMAS.

Whilst we are here we had better recall the

tale which the poet Taylor tells about th wily old man. "He had had three leases in the house and all had expired. He took his last lease of his landlord, one Master John Porter, for life, with which lease he had lived more than fifty years, but the old man would for his wife's sake, renew his lease for years, which his landlord would not consent thereto; wherefore old Parr having been long blind ' sitting in his chair by the fire, his wife looked out at the windows, and perceived Master Edward Porter, the son of his landlord, to come towards their house, which she told her husband, saying, 'Husband, our young land- lord is coming hither." Is he so' said old Parr; ' I pr'ythee, wife, lay a pin on the ground near my foot, or at my right toe,' which she did; and when young Master Porter, yet forty years old was come into the house, after salutations between them, the old man said, ` Wife is not that a pin which lies at my foot ? " Truly husband,' quoth she 'it is a pin indeed.' So she took up the pin, and Master Porter was half in a maze that the old man had recovered his sight again; but it was quickly found to be a witty conceit thereby to have them believe him to be more lively than he was, because he hoped to have his lease renewed for his wife's sake as aforesaid." Which shows that Parr did not live his century for nothing.

===== 0002601_19110916_art0076.txt | included=true | included =====
MARRIED AT A HUNDRED AND TWENTY.

But we are forgetting to give dates. John Parr, the father of the famous Thomas, was an agricultural labourer in Alberbury and it was in 1483 that his son was born. Thomas through his long life followed his father's calling. Until the age of 80 he was a bachelor and then married his first wife with whom he lived 32 years. At the reputed age of 120 Ihe married for the second time.

His life seems to have been uneventful enough. When presented to Charles I. the king said " You have lived longer than other men, what have you done more than other men." Parr's reply was "I did penance when I was a hundred years old." The thought of Parr, aged 150, standing before the king, reminds one of Jacob and Pharoah, but it is pitiful to read his anwser. '1 he explanation is that he had been forced by the clergy to do penance as punishment for an intrigue with Catherine Milton, the woman who afterwards became his second wife. The penance consisted in standing in a white stu,et at the door of Alberbury Church.

===== 0002601_19110916_art0077.txt | included=true | included =====
lIIS TRIP TO LONDON.

I” the year 1635 Parr was reputed to have reached the age of 152 and had been visited by the Earl of Arundel. His lordship was struck by his appearance and induced him to pay a visit to London. Parr agreed and the Earl set himself to provide for the old man's comfdll. This is the record of his effort. "He commanded a litter and two horses (for the more easy carriage of a man so enfeebled and worn with age) to he provided for him; also that a daughter- in-law of his (named Lucye) should likewise attend him, and have a horse for her owne riding with him; and to cheere up the olde man, and make him merry, there was an antique faced fellow, called Jacke, or John the Foote, with a high and mighty no beard, that had also a horse for his carriage; and likewise one of his honours own servants, named Brian Kelly, to ride on horseback and to attend and defray all manner of rejoicings and expenses; all which was done accordingly." •

===== 0002601_19110916_art0078.txt | included=true | included =====
BURIED IN WESTMINSTER ABBEY.

We can picture the cavalcade leaving the old house for London. The old man left it never to return. It would have been far better had Earl Arundel resisted the temptation to make a "lion' of him. Owing partly to the fatigue of the journey and the unusual and exciting life in the capital with the crowds of visitors who flocked to see him, Parr fell ill and died. The famous physician Harvey examined his body and declared it most healthy and without any trace of decay or disease. Had he been left in the pure air of Shropshire he might have lasted many years. He was laid to rest in Westminster Abbey on November 15th, 1655, and his monument may still be seen there. It is a curious thought that the tenant of this thatched house went straight to a grave in the Abbey. Surely no man ever got a place more easily.

A NEW TORY PARTY

Why Not Call It - The Antique

Bantams '' ?

There are in Nature certain organisms, described as" fissiparous," that regularly undergo a process of splitting up into parts and seem to enjoy it. That appears to be the condition of the Tory party just now, for the announcement was made on Tuesday evening that a nem party, or association, is to be formed, whirls is ta be " auxiliary to the present Conservative forces, " and will, if all goes well, have Lord Halsbury, the " antique bantam" ;of the " Morning Poet, " for its first president. It would make a not too easy test in political knowledge to be asked at a moment's notice the title of the different Tory parties that bare been, or recently were, actively engaged in political warfare against each other. There is, of courue, the great Tory party itself, the fissiparous parent of the others; but there were, also, the" Die Dards," an extreme faction of whom were known as " Last Ditchers." Then there was a section that began as "Die flards," and ended by earning for themselves, in the Tory clubs, the only title by which they are known to p litical history that of "Trait irs. '

To go a little further back, there were the Tory Tariff Reformers and the Tory Free Traders, and the former were split up into" Whole flogger*" and mere Preferentialists. There have also been Tory Home Rulers, who call themselves "Devolutionist.," and whose chief (to his sorrow now) was Mr. George Wyndham. The circular in which the formation of the latest party is announced is addrevied to L-ad flalsbury, land is u follows . I We, the undersigned, desire to express to your lordship our high appreciation of the consistent attitude maintained by you in opposition to the ' Constitution wreckers. We welcome the new spirit infused into the Unionist party, fired by the notion of yourself and your colleaguea. We desire thM. the movement then insugutsted shell be perpetuated, and 1,0 kelp towards this cud propose to fqrm ourselves into an association, not in opposition to, but nutiliary to, the present Conservative forms. We trust you sill h moor the usociation by becoming our first president. We welcome the new party, and, as to still with- out a name, we should suggest that it .hould call itself " The Antique Bantams." " B. N. C.'.

I saw her in a passing omnibus, and climbed up to send your Excel- lency a message—when I returned the young lady and gentleman had parted. I have not seen Mr. Lomat&le. since." "Enough," said the Mirza, speaking in great anger. "You have utterly failed. I have other servants who in future will attend to my business. Oct ready to leave London to.clay." _ _ _

A look e once of relief and despair came like a flash on the Easter's face.

"Yon will go to Marseilles and put yourself into the hands of Seleucus. He will need you. On the faithfulness with which you discharge the duties he will set you to execute, your chance of life depends. Get ready; you are useless in London. I will take this matter into my own hands." The man salaamed obediently and left the Mirzia presence. In a quarter of an hour the great Mire. was driving abroad in his carriage. His Eastern servants drove his horses through the Lon- don streets. Their dress, rich with all the pomp of Persia, attracted attention. The horses were coal black, and most magnificent animals. The Mirza, unaccompanied, was seated in the carriage. He was dressed like an Englishman, except for his fez. His face was very dark, very inscrutable. The carriage presently drew up outside the house of Lady Jane Halliday. One of the servants sprang from the box and rang the bell. An elderly servant in Lady Jane's claret coloured livery flung the door open. The other servant bowed and asked if Lady Jane Halliday would grant the Mirza Kara Yusuf , a moment's interview. .

George, the footman, said he would in- quire, and went upstairs to Lady Jane's dra wing-room. Now, it was one thing for Lady Jane Halli- day to offer a refuge and home to Rebecca Henshall, and another thing to be denied any share in the girl's secret. She was the kindest of women, but by no means the wisest. She had been deeply interested by Silver's account of the dark Eastern servant who had brought a letter to Miss Henshall, and who had got such a curt dismissal from the young lady. Lady Jane sat in her drawing-room, tiad earnestly wished that Rebecca would COMP downstairs. Rebecca remained in the dress- ing-room with her door locked. Lady Jane hoped that Mrs. Lorrimer would call. She would not betray dear Rebecca for the world, but she might hint to her friend that this visit on the part of the girl was con- nected with the mystery. Then there came the sound of horses' hoofs in the street below, and the sudden cessation of noise out- side her door, followed a minute or two later by the entrance of George with a card on a salver. "His Excellency begs to see you for a couple of minutes, my lady." Lady Jane felt her heart beat. A Persian —a If ins—a man next to the Shah in rank and dignity! He was calling upon her ! This was really exciting. Of course she would see

him. "Take down my compliments, and say I shall be pleased to give the Mires an inter- view," said the lady. George withdrew, and presently the dark and handsome Oriental entered Lady Jane's presence. He bowed to her, and she bowed to him. She asked him to be seated, and he sank gracefully into an easy chair. Lady Jane looked at him with increasing pleasure and appreciation. How foreign he was how striking! how handsome! She wondered if she might secure him for one of her "at homes." He would be the lion of the party. But what had he come to see her about? She waited for him to speak. , An Oriental is not true to himself if he hut ries. The Mirza was always prompt in action, but he never hurried. He waited now calmly for Lady Jane Halliday to get over her astonishment. Meanwhile, he was taking the lady in, measuring her, and assuring himself that he could easily get so foolish a woman into his power. "To what do I owe the pleasure of this visit?" said Lady Jane then. And the Mirza spoke at once. "You will pardon me, I hope, Lady Jane Halliday," he said in his flute-like voice, his enunciation very slow, his English perfect, "but I have come here to make a request of you." "Anything in my power," said Lady Jane. looking into those marvellous, deep eyes, and then feeling constrained to look away. "I know it, dear lady," said the Mirza. "The moment I saw your face I felt sure that you were gentle as the dove and wise as the serpent. I have only ventured to trouble you to make a few inquiries. You have • young lady staying at the present moment in your house of the name of Henshall—Miss Re- becca Menshall, of 9, Paddock Row." "What of that?" asked Lady Jane, who felt that she must be careful, and yet could not see why she need deny the fact that Re- becca was visiting her. "I am anxious to have an interview with

that young lady, said the 'lima. "How very interesting !" thought Lady Jane. She could not possibly connect danger with the fact that so gracious, handsome, wonderful a man wanted to see her friend. Still, however, she felt herself hesitating. The Mims read her thoughts as "clearly as though she had spoken them aloud. He was a profound thought-reader, and prided him- self on the fact. " Madam." he said very gently. "I base a most important message to give Miss Re- b.scs lie•uamil. hut the fact hi. She 15 afraid

one who WOUla be Der beet Menu.-

- "Is it possible?" cried Lady Jane. "Yes' dear madam," said the Mirza, speak- ing in the saddest of voices and putting on • pathetic smile that would melt a heart of stone, "it is true. I have written twice to the young lady, requesting the pleasure of her company in order to do her an inestimable service, and she wili not even reply to my letter. She is afraid of me, but without cause. If she were to see me, everything would come right." "I am very much puzzled," said Lady Jane. "I cannot understand Rebecca's rude- ness. It really does seem rude not to have even answered your letter. Did you say you wrote twice?"

"Yes, madam ; once to her own house, and once I took the liberty of addressing a letter here."

"Oh—then," said Lady Jane, "it was your servant who brought that letter! My maid, Silver, was very much excited about it." "Perhaps, madam," said the Mirza, "you could induce Miss Henshall to change her mind, and to give me a short interview." "Here—do you mean?" asked Lady Jane. The Mirza considered. "I think not," he said then. "I can best convey my meaning to her in my own private suite of apartments at the Carlton Hotel. Can you induce her to accompany you there, madam? I should not dream of seeing the young lady, except in your presence. With you by, I would convey to her the warning which is necessary for her safety." "Oh, then," said Lady Jane, taken com- pletely off her guard, "you•are aware of her danger?" "1 know of it but too well." "It is real, then?" said Lady Jane. "Very real," replied tile Mina, Thiry im min

'ant, very certain." I love her like a daughter," said Lady Jane then, "and would do anything in the world for her. I cannot understand her refusal to see you, or even to reply to your gracious letters. She must altogether mistake your intentions." "She does. Her danger springs from a source which I alone can turn aside—a word with her will suffice. I must have that word. May I ask you and Miss Henshall to honour me by drinking tea with me to-day at the Carlton Hotel. I trust to you, Lady Jane Halliday, to use your persuasions with Miss Henshall. If you cannot bring her, will you come alone? But I shall hope to see you both." . _ .

"I will do my utmost to bring Rebecca," said Lady Jane, "and in any case / shall be delighted to go. lam much interested in Persia, and 1 am certain that you can tell me delightful stories about it. PALACE AND HIPPODROME.

Crowded audiences have been the rule again this week at the popular Palace and the programme has given general sati,faction, though, in our view, the entertainment was completely spoil( d en Monday by the vulgarity of an 'eccentric comedian" whose jokes were mostly a medium f.a- indelicate suggestion. Edward Crosland's Melody Makers were a most enjoy- able contrast. They combined mirth and melody with real skill, the part.songs being excellently rendered. Their scene is entitled " the Burglar's Deans," and the burglar has a very trying time when surrounded by capering goblins wh9 overwhelm him with octaves, cadenzas and the like. Sandy McNab is a genuine Scotch comedian who has struck a new vein of humour in he sketch " King Pharaoh." Sandy fills the roIC (not roll) of a mummy and is handed over to a half mad physician who proposes ta experiment on him with the" elixir of He." Sandy, for a consideration, mummifies himself and occupies a very Egyptian- looking coffin labelled "King Pharoah." The scene which follows between himself, the Professor and the latter's daughter reduced the audience to paroxysms of laughter, and was the feature of the evening. A very smart performance is given by The Ives, brother and sister, in ballpunehing— a physical recreation which looks e.isy, but is exceedingly difficult. The Gilbert sinters excelled as simultaneous dancers, but would be well-advised to " cut " the vocal part of the turn, as singing is not their forte. Dolly Rees was well received in her ballads and coon songs, and a singing and dancing duo by Barrie and Wood came in for generous recognition.

FROM LABOUR'S STANDPOINT.

By Philip Snowden, M.P. THE TRADES CONGRESS AND STRIKES.

The Trades Congress this year has been very disappointing. It may be that too great expectations had been formed as to the lead which would be given to the workers. Be that as it may the fact now remains that the Congress has given no had and the workers are left to direct their discontent as they may think fit, or as they may be influenced by outsiders who have assumed the leadership in the absence of direction from the responsible officials of the unions. The only thing the Congress did in regard to labour disputes was to unanimously censure Will Crooks, who had at least tried to do something to deal with labour difficulties, however unfortunately be had blundered in the effort. There are many things in Mr. Crooks's Bill which are objectionable, but it is perfectly certain that not one in twenty of the delegates who censured the amiable member for Woolwich has ever seen a copy of the Bill, much less given it a careful study. The trades unions have got to say plain and square whether they are in favour of Con- ciliation or not. The general tenour of the speeches seemed to convey the impression that even the old leaders have gone back to reliance on the strike as the most effective means of securing concessions. That point should have been made clear by the Congress, but it was left in doubt. A STRUGGLE AHEAD.

We are in for a fight for the preservation of the rights which trade unions have won by a century of agitation. The right to picket is being attacked. Efforts will be made to induce Parliament to repeal the last Trades Union Act. The trades unions can best put themselves right with the public by showing that they have no desire to inflict all the inconvenience and suffering of a strike on the community except as a last resort. It is a good policy in warfare to put the responsi- bility for forcing the fight upon one's adver- sary. The strength of the railway men's position in the recent strike lay in the fact that it was the companies who forced the men to that extremity by refusing to meet the men to amicably discuss the differences. There seems to be a disposition in some quarters to adopt a policy of what we might call surprise strikes. That is to say, for the workmen to keep quiet until a favourable moment comes and then without notice or warning to strike. However much the men might gain from such tactics it is perfectly certain they would lose infinitely more. They might succeed once or twice, but their action would arouse such intense public indignation that disastrous reprisals would follow.

THE SECTIONAL STRIKE USELESS. There is talk of a universal strike in the coal trade. There is something to be said for that, whereas there. is nothing to be said in

favour of the sectional strike in the coal

industry. Every sectional strike of anyil magnitude in the coal trade has ended in defeat for the men for years back. It must I be so. In a partial strike the men who are working elsewhere are killing the chances of success for their mates on strike. We have got to face this fact, that if there are going to be strikes in the future they are going to be strikes on a wild scale, and of a character which will inflict widespread and enormous inconvenience and suffer- ing. The men should always be in a strong position when they strike. They should always be in the right. They never will hold a strong position in the opinion of the public unless they have tried all peaceful means to get their grievances redressed before resorting to a strike. If it has to coma to a strike it is better that it

should be on such a scale as to bring the industry to a standstill, for then it cannot last long, and instead of long-drawn-out suffering with certain defeat in the end, we get a state of things so intolerable that a speedy settlement is certain. A universal strike in the coal trade, for instance, couldn't last for more than a few days. A few days stoppage would bring the railway service and all the factories in the country to a stand- still. Horrible as the inconvenience and suffering of such a strike would be it would not inflict as much suffering as is caused by such a strike as the one just ended in the Rhondda, where thousands of families have been on starvation rations for nearly a year.

One other thing that such a strike its is

'contemplated in „-the coal trade would do is to put an end to strikes. Just as the terrible character of modern warfare is one of the strongest arguments in favour of peace, so the appalling consequences of a national strike would drive the community to adopt some measures to protect itself from such catastrophes. The Trades Union Congress firmly held by the right of workmen to strike. But the right to strike is conditional on circumstances. It is an anti-social act for any section to seek its own ends by inflicting suffering on the general body of citizens. On the other hand it is an unjustifiable thing for the majority to deny to the minority adequate means for expressing their discontent and for obtaining their just demands. If that right to obtain justice be not conceded then revolt is justifi- able. The railway strike again illustrates this important point. The responsibility for that strike lay in the first degree with the railway directors, and in the second degree with the State. The State was responsible for seeing that the railway men had adequate means for pressing for what they considered their just demands. The State did not pro vide the men with these facilities, Therefor the State, having failed in its duty to th men, the men were absolved from responsi- bility for the anti-social consequences of their action in striking. AN UNTENABLE CONTENTION.

The right to strike can only exist so long as there are no other effective means of enforcing the reasonable demands of labour. And finally, the decision as to whether the demands put forward by any section of the workers are reasonable must rest with the community, or in other words with the State. There can be no appeal beyond the court of representatives of the community. That seems to me to be the abstract argument in favour of compulsory State arbitration. I am convinced that a few such experiences as those through which we have recently passed will force State arbitration. And I should like this to come not in the face of the opposition of trade unionism. but with its help and approval. It is certain that if the trade unions continue to maintain an attitude of hostility to all forms of State Arbitration, and assert the right to strike when they think fit,that there will be imposed upon them with the general assent of the community,some system of State Arbitration which will be most unfair and unacceptable. I am sure that the workers who have gained advances of wages by the recent strikes would have had equal advances

granted by any impartial and competent Court of Arbitration. The experience of the working of such courts in the colonies supports that opinion. Trade Unionists look shy at Arbitration and Conciliation Boards now because of their constitution. I do not think there is one responsible trade union leader in the cotton trade but would agree that the wages in that industry would be lower than they are to-day if the employers and the workpeople had fought out these matters by the strike and lockout during the last twenty years. The trade union move- ment will, I feel sure, make a tremendous mistake if it does not set to work to devise some scheme for settling wages questions when we have passed out of the present tem- porary epoch in which the strike idea is dom- inating. This is a pleasing phase. It has no permanent value except in so far as it gets the workers thinking about their conditions, • and teaches them the lesson that permanent improvement will come by united action put forth through constitutional channels and by political methods. A proletariat which has not learnt to vote solidly has not yet grasped the first purpose to which the solidarity idea should be supplied. It would be destructive of all chance of solid progress if the working classes were to get the idea that the strike is the easy way to salvation. It is not. In the present state of social evolution it may he necessary as an occasional method, just as war may be a regrettable necessity; but just as inter- national diplomacy marks a higher stage of human progress than war so the reference of industrial disputes to the arbitration of an impartial tribunal is a step in advance which it should be our desire to take.

THE RA IN-STORMs.-013 Tuesday evening the village was visited by a violent downpour of rain, which lasted /or several hours. The darkness descended at 5.45 p.m. very swiftly. The rain has saved the crops damage, and has been very welcome. It was one of Lb, most hsavit at downpours kno n for several years. WESLEYAN CHURCH.-00 Sunday last, the Services at this church were conducted by the Rev. W. Nicholson the nearly appointed minister, who is undertaking this district as part of his labours. He preached two sermons to a well filled church, both being mach appreciated. Mr. Nicholson hopes to preach in this church every third Sunday.

CONGREGATIONAL CHRISTIAN SERVICE HAND.—A good number of young people assembled at this meet- ing on Monday evening. An exvellent address was given by Mr. Sydney Linley, on the great misery and evil ceased by the curse of "Drink." A solo was rendered by Mr. Alfred Moines entitled" Precious Words," in a most credit tble manner. Mr. Harry Rushton presided. The meeting concluded with several short prayers. _ .

FUNERAL OF Mn. J. A. lIATTON.—Great interest was taken in the district on Sunday afternoon, when the mutiny of Mr. John Arthur Hatton, son of Mr. John Hatton, butcher, Booth's lull, Lymm, were interred at the Parish Church. Mr. Batton very recently took up businees in Cadishead and lately net with a serious injury through over-lifting, thur causing an early death. Ile was only 33 years of age and great sympathy in felt for his wife and children.

WI:DOM.—A pretty wedding took place on Wednes- day afternoon in the Congregational Church. when Mr. John Rose, son of Mr. William Rose, Eagle Brow, Lymm, was married to Miss Elsie Turner, daughter of Mr. John Turner, of Latchford. There was a nice con- gregation present at the ceremony which was conducted by the Rev. R. W. B. Whiteway, of Lymm. The bride who was given away by her father, wore a blue dress and a white hat trimmed with a large ostrich feather, looked quite a picture of happiness. The, were after- wards driven away Iv motor and held their reception at the tridegrooms house, afterwards leaving for their honeymocn. PRIESENTATIOS TO MR. J. Rosit—On Sunday after- noon, the tesche-s and elder scholars of the Congrega- tional Sunday School met ia the vestry to make a presentation of an oak clock to Mr. John Rose, to celebrate his coming marriage. Mr. Rose bits been a scholar and teacher in the Sunday school for a good number of years. The presentation was made by Mr. J. H. Holt, the superintendent, who said in handing the clock to Mr. Rose that they were losing a good teacher, one who had stuck to his sehool and had tried to fulfil his duties in every way. They hoped that he would be happy in his sphere of labour, and a'so that his wife-to-be and himself would not forget their friends in Lymm. Mr. Rme in reply- ing said he was sorry to part from such good friends and teachers. He hoped to join another school in his new destination and ahould visit them often. He very heartily thanked the teachers and friends for so nice and useful a present Mr. J. D. B. Smith also spoke a few words and wished Mr. Rose success and happiness in his new work and home. The hymn 'God be with you till we meet again " was sung on departing.

There MS a large audience of workingmen to hear Mr. Tom Mann in the Borough Rink, Widnes, on Wednesday awning, the occasion arising out of the dock dispute at Wickes. . .

Mr. M,ann said he was chiefly concerned abcut the organisation of the workers. He hoped to convince them of the desirability of orrianisina, and of the brnelits co ruitsg tram a c wubtned eff ,rt on the part of working men. He wanted them to realise the humdrum character of their lives, if they had regular work, in or about Widnes, the wages they received, and the standard of life that such admitted, such wages being far below what intelligent men and women in the world said they should enjoy. Poverty would remain with them is-d intensify if they continued to remain apathetic. Clues war was a fact; and they were fully prepare] to range themselv,s on the side of that class which would remove the bad conditions and effect improvements. They had got to learn the wisdom of organisation, which would enable them to assert their position. When they did that poverty would vanish altogether ; there would be no room for the idle classes. lie the speaker) said be always gee primary attention to the bottom dog—the man with a family—who got the list enjoyment. There were many who did not get 21s. per week, and he was determined that his eft rrts would not cease until every working man was paid £2 per work. Upon a show of hands expressing sympathy with the union several hundred hands went up•

The Examiner Saturday September 16, 1911

===== 0002601_19110916_art0105.txt | included=true | included =====
IRISH AFFAIRS. J NOTES FOR IRISH READERS. a

TILE HOME RULE CAMPAIGN.

An Ulster Unionist journal tells us this week of the perfected arrangements for the organisation of the country "from the Unionist side "in reference to Home Rule. But a little while ago this statement might have been taken to mean that a plentiful supply of arms had been received and dis- tributed in the Orange lodges and that mid- night drillings and manceuvres in the glens of Antrim were increasing in military efficiency. But no such sensation appears in this recent announcement. It is merely a statement that with the object of preparing a campaign against Home Rule, a Joint Committee has been formed under the supervision and direction of the ill-organised Conservative Central Office in London. No details of the Joint Committee's crusade are vouchsafed, but it is not difficult to foreshadow the operations of this body. On platforms from Cork to Antrim and from Lands End to John-o-Groats the paid agitators of a small re-actionary faction will reiterate stale slanders in the hope that the ignorance and prejudice which influenced the British masses eighty years ago will still avail the forces of reaction.

A foretaste of the fictions, we had nearly written "arguments," is furnished in an article in the " Standard " designed to shew to the English people that the granting of Home Rule means a giving over of the country to separation advocates and fierce religious intolerance. " There is plenty of evidence" it is stated, without any evidence being adduced " that the old-cry has not been dropped, but that it is, indeed, being expressed quite as violently as in the past." The argument is manifestly dishonest. The sentiment of the people is expressed by their representatives in the Irish party. The voice of Nationalist Ireland is heard without any warring note of dissonance in the United Irish League and by the Irish party is the claim of Ireland to legisla- tive freedom authoritatively urged. Not one of the responsible leaders of Nationalist Ireland, Butt, Parnell, or Redmond can be cited in support of the slander. Andiapropos of the religious fiction we wonder why these e latant orators do not consider some of the convincing utterances of certain leading Protestants. One such utter- ance is of special interest at the moment. Sir David Harrel, it will be borne in mind, is the Chairman of the Railway Commission, whose judgment and fair-mindedness the forces of Capital and Labour in the recent Railway strike agreed to accept in his final decision on the root questions at issue. Sir David Harrel has a unique experience of Ireland, and the conditions obtaining in every part of the country. And this is the view on the religious point which this Government official, professing the Protestant faith, has recently expressed in circumstances of the fullest publicity : "My conviction is that a change in the government of Ireland, provided that change be conceived and carried out on sound financial principles, would not adversely ' affect the position of religious minorities. Speaking of the Land Acts and the Local Government Act, Sir David continues "It must be admitted that these great respon- sibilities have been accepted with reasonable- ness and restraint. Greater freedom with its attendant greater responsibilities would raise a situation which Irishmen could meet without misgiving, but their task must not be made impossible by inadequate resources. As regards the gener- osity of Irish Catholics, I have a title to speak. I owe it entirely to non-official Catholic friends that I was placed in positions of trust and responsibility, in which it has been my privilege to contribute to those changes which have been a benefit to my fellow-countrymen. I do not know that I have ever had anything to recommend me beyond a deep sympathy for the cheerless, often hopeless, condition of the small tenant I farmer; his interests and position still remain a matter of sincere concern to me." This is the considered judgment of a leading Irish Protestant, after a lifetime of service in the Irish Constabulary, and whose ability has gained the distinction we just referred to. We have ventured to draw attention to these points because we desire to suggest to Liberal and Nationalist alike that in the impending fight the representatives of Ireland will have ranged against them the most rabid of anti-Irish forces and that Ireland in her comparative weakness will need the support of every lover of clean fighting and fair play. Appeals to race hatred and religious prejudice are already being made by Unionist agitators in all parts of Great Britain, and since the work is so well-endowed, the befouling of Ireland and her people will be long sustained. . .

For these reasons we are delighted to learn of the tour of the Eighty Club, which commences on the 15th instant, throughout the four provinces of Ireland. The Eighty Club, as is known, supplies most of the speakers for the Liberal propaganda meet- ings and by reason of the personnel of its members and the wide area of .its operations , is an organisation of the first importance. The ' party will include many prominent Liberals and will be in Ireland just over a fortnight, visiting Dublin, Belfast, Cork, Limerilk, Galway and other provincial centres,as well as several of the congested districts. They will be received by Mr John Redmond on their arrival and, they will then proceed to hear from Nationalist and Unionist repre- sentative men, opinions and views on the several phases of the Irish question. Every- where Nationalists are actively preparing to give the fullest facilities to these Englishmen who are anxious to see for themselves the actual position of affairs in Ireland. It is a happy inspiration on the part of the Eighty Club and we are sure that the tour will be rich in results which cannot fail to be of the highest value in the approaching campaign.

CONSUMPTIVE PATIENTS. SUGGESTED ARRANVEMENT WITH IHE COO PORATION. The Medical Officers reported that at present the consumptive patients Who were able to he out of bed in the day time, had no day room to sit in. They, in consequence, had to sit in the ward beside the patients who were in an advanced state of the disease and not able to he hp. 1 hat was moat ot jectionahle to patients who were very ill, as well as harmful to those ions earlier stage. As a remedy for that they would suggest the erection of a cheap wooden shelter ass day room for those who were able to sit tip. It was recommended that the matter he referred to the Building Committee with request to report at an early date.

The minutes were confirmed. As interesting discussion arose on a suggestion to send a consumptive patient chargeable to the Board to a sanatorium at Sandgate, Kent. Mr. Ft" FINESS said he was in hopes that an arrange- ment could be made with the Health Committee of the Corporation whereby consumptive cases from the Union could be dealt with at the Senkey Sanatorium. If cases could be treated locally it would be far preferable. I Mr. EVAN-, as chairman of the Health Committee of the Corporation, said it would be premature on his part I to ray very much on the subject. The Corporation was not operating through the Sankey Sanatorium from a relief point of vise. hut simply with a regard to the I poblic health. The Guardians, on the other hand, were working from the relief point of view. There at as a I great deal in what Mr. Furness mid. If by a scheme of cohesion and co-operation between the Cl' uardians and the health authorities they could arrange for the removal of early cases or of patients who were living in dire poverty or in congested conditions, it would probably he to the adventage of both bodies. How tar the Health Committee would be able to deal with the question-at Sankey he scarcely knew, because they found that in the town Rust( there were a large- number of persons affected by the disease than the public generally dreamed of. They had already removed fourteen or eighteen cases, and in all proba- bility-some of them would eventually have come to the Guardians. From a health point of view, the Health Committee had taken the initiative and removed the patients. Behind it all, there was the possibility of considerable overlapping. While he did not personally think it was the duty of the health authorities to relieve the Guardians of their tesponsibility, he thought the Guardians could in some measore co-operate with them and the basis of payment could easily he arrangedit was a coming subject, and a very important one. If they did not make an arrange- ment, they were lettieg their money go into other comsties to assist outside authorities to maintain their institutious. ST. HELENS.

ST. HELENS FARM BLAZE. MOTOR-ENGINE TESTED.

Ile St. Helens Fire Brigaee were called early on Sunday morning to an outbreak at Sutton Side Farm, (Baton-lane, where a stack of unthrestied oats sod a stack of hay, belonging to Mr. Thomas Cook, were burning vigorously. The stacks were very near a large shippon, which the brigade, however, were successful io ening. Superintendent Shaw turned the large new motor-engine out for the first time to a farm fire, and though it had to cross fluids to get to the nearest pool of water, the engine did all that was required in the most satisfactory way. The outbreak was soon extin- guished, but the flames had involved the whole of the stacks and did damage to the amount of .£l5O, which is covered by insurance.

NEW THATTO HEATH LIBRARY. MR. CARNEGIE THANKED.

At a meeting of the St. Helens Library Committee, on Monday, Alderman Martin, chairman, read a letter that he had addresind to Mr. Andrew Carnegie, pointing out the inadequate accommodation at the Thatto Heath branch, and suing for £3,000 for the erection of a rew library. Mr. Cernegie has replied promihisg the £3,800 I to be expended solely on the library building. The present branch library was opened in November, 1891. Mr. Carnegie some few years ago gave money far building new branch libraries at button end Eccleston. On the motion of Alderman Martin, seconded by Mr. Fletcher, Alderman Foote, Colonel Thome', snd Mr. fleslam Fox, a hearty vote of thanks was paised to Mr. Carnegie. ST. HELENS STRIKE AVERTED. COLLIERS AND "DIFFICULT PLACES."

The position of affairs with regard to the terms of employment of meo in the Raveohead Pit of the St. Helen. Collieriee Iran discussed ot WedoeadaY by Messrs. Arnold and Orchard, managers, and Messrs T Glover, Ashton nod T Oreenall, of the Miners' Federatic n. .

A small number of men had ceased work as a protest against the small amounts which they said it ws• only possible for them to earn in difficult plaors in the working'. The colliers had been balloted, and by a huge majority had decided to strike if better terms were not given. Tbe trouble never got very acute, for the management had found places in good seams far the mei who had gone out, and Wednesday's conference amicably settled matters, it being stated that all the men were now in good work. The following day one of Awe ‘'ten applied for work. He was t)141. however, that no man need apply for work again if Ire was over 35 years of age. Anyhow the same day arrothvr was started.who was older than the first applicint. 4" Recently a vacancy for a shunter occurred. An extra man who bad been out on strike applied. Be want elel that no extra men wood ever be made permanent bands. When a man feels that he is being imposed upon sou begins to ask Why should I be? be is told that if he goes in front of the ' Rummer ' he need never espect to start on the railway again. And these are the words used by a man who is supposed to manipulate traffic. When the strikers mat in I thought it was an ucderetocd thieg that every man was to be reinstated 'rand not subjected to intimidation. If stich things are carried in. a second strike in a very short time would not surprise ,curs faithfully C. I. F. Mr. -New and His Critics. Yours very truly, HARRY NEW. 62, Norris-street, Sept. 15th 1011.

" Through De2th Valley."

The Mormon peril has aroused widespread attention of late and now we have a piny dealing with the subject Mr. Joseph Le Brandt's em iiing Urania " Through Death Valley," sheds • lurid light on life in Salt Lake City and if one tithe of what is alleged against the profet,sors of Mormonism in the West is true. we cannot wonder at the increasing repugnance with which this form of" religion "is regarded. Polygamy is only one of the Fins which the author lays to the charge of Nosh Catchpole, the scheming elder of the Mormon Church, and his accomplices. Their career,as depicted in the play, is one long record of treachety and double-dealing, culminating in minder. Judged to its merits as a diamatic production, the work is an exceptionally powerful one, intereet never flagging for a moment, whi'e there is a surfeit of sensations which makes the audience bold its bi Kith Particularly is this the case when the hero finds himself tied to a bee, with a rattle snake ass companion, but before the reptile has time to begin its amiable attentions, the faithful Indian Grey Wolf, always on the scene at the right moment, dis- patches it with a bullet. Mr. Jack Fo, tescue makes a dashing, monly Jim blyddleton, the miner hero. ALDERMANIC SENSATION.

Interesting Position.

[TO THE EDITOR OF THE " EXAMINER.I

Sir,—Seeing notices in the Press re lapse of my qualification as a burgess and intimating that thts will cause a vacant seat on the Aldermanic Bench, I desire to say that it is not my intention to resign from the Council. The lapse of my past qualification had quite escaped my memory till too late to be rectified, otherwise I should have seen to the holding of another qualification. On consult- ing the Municipal Corporations AO, I do not consider that there is reason for me to resign, and it is my intention to follow the course adopted by the late Alderman John Pick. mere, who found himself, throtigh oversight, exactly in the same position as I myself am to-day.—Yours truly

W. PIERPOINT.

Trautenau, Latchford.

13th September. 1911.

The above letter from Alderman Pierpoint will be read with satisfaction by his many friends and admirers in view of the premature announcement diligently circulated in Tory quarters that, as an outcome of the proceed- ings in the Revision Court, he was about to retire from the Town Council. Alderman Pierpoint is fully justified in the position he has taken up. It is true that, as his claim to a vote was not sustained, be loses his qualification as a burgess and his name will not appear on the new list which comes into operation on November Ist. He is perfectly at liberty to act as an alderman until that date and, as the term for which he was elected does not expire until 1916, he can still continue to hold office, though not to discharge the duties, until such time as he again qualifies as a burgess. There is one important local precedent for an alderman, disqualified in precisely the same way as Alderman Pierpoint, retain- , iqg office. The late Alderman John Pffikmere lost hisJote under similar circum- stances hut did nbi resign, and resumed his duties after he had acquired a nualification the following year.. If Alderman Pierpoint pursues the same course his seat cannot be declared vacant by two burgesses, as is the custom in case of resignation, and the Town Council ;:titself cannot take any action in the matter. The only means by which the legality of his position could ne contested is by an action brought by a burgess in the High Court. Until he again secures a qualification, however, Alderman Pierpoint will be debarred from voting or from taking part in discussions ,in the Council. We hear that in anticipation of a vacancy being declared in November. the Tories had decided to push Councillor W. Rogers claim to the aldermanic seat, although he is second to Councillor Wilkinson in seniority.

FRED TAYLOR

By One Who Knew Him

. . . acd loved him too, for that was the natural sequence if you were fortunate enough to know him well. But you did not get to know him all at once. There was a certain shyness of manner which made one appreciate more fully the privilege of being able to penetrate beyond it ; and the deeper one got the more profound was the respect and admiration for what one found. There was no fuss or ostenta- tion, his strength was in his quietness. Can you , see Lim—all ycit friends of his? I can ' picture him most Joyfully ! How he found pleasure in simple things, because his mini was broad, and he saw the best in life ! How courteous was his manner, and how kind and considerate he was to all ! His smile and kindly greeting to those about made him a most welcome visitor in any house. Whether at work or at play his influence mmrt have been for good. You never heard him make a dis- paraging remark about anyone, he seemed always to see the good in everyone, and he ever drew the best from us. There was strength and power for good in him, which must have given inspiration to ethers; yet he could be so gentle and tender, and was always so tactful and unselfish He was a moat delightful companion in travel. He had red widely, and remembered 1. hat he read ' • he had a great store of information—historic and legendary— sod his sense of humour, keen observation, willingness to fall in with any plans, and the ahsolute joy and satir.faction be found in everything, made him most delightful and interesting. It was splendid to see him with children ; he was great enough to become a little child with them. Nothing in nature tamped him, for he was a true lover of the countr- The

for he wus _ .ortr ot toe Lu...ry. - light-hearted happiness of his la4t holiday with us, amid the beauty of hill and vale, will ever be one of our most cherished recollemions.

" Hie life was gentle: and the elements so mixed in him, that Nature might stand up, and any to all the world ' This was a man."

HOWLEY BRIDGE.

TENDER ACCEPTED AND WORK TO PROCEED

Residents of the Howley and Latchford Wards will be gratified to learn that there is an early prospect of the much-talked of bridge across the Mersey to connect the two districts becoming an accomplished fact. At a meeting of the Bridges Committee yesterday a plan was approved and tender accepted for the construction of a footbridge , at Howley, and the Borough Surveyor was instructed to proceed at once with the laying of the foundations on the I Victoria Park side. We understand that the cost of the structure will not exceed £4OO. When completed it will prove an untold convenience to the public, as they will be enabled to cross from Howley to Latchford without making the circuitous journey thr9ugh the town. Bowlers in particular will appreciate the improved means of getting to the greens in Victoria Park.

Although not on a very pretentious scale, the new bridge will, we are informed, be of pleasing design, the ornamental as well as the merely utilitarian aspect having received due attention.

===== 0002601_19110916_art0129.txt | included=true | included =====
WARRINGTON BRIDGE.

Subject to the approval of the Ccuncil the Bridges Committee has accepted a tender for the construction of a temporary footway Gver the eastern side of the existing bridge. The provision of this accommodation for pedestrians will be rendered necessary by the closing of one of the footpaths while the bridge works are in progress. In order to facilitate the traffic over the footpaths on each side of the iron bridge which spans the ' Mersey at Arpley, the Borough Surveyor has been instructed to request the London and North- Western Railway Company to remove tlie small gates and thus allow a continuous stream of pedestrians to pars along without having, as at present, t 3 wait their turns at a narrow gateway.
